### Question 051: Epigenetic Regulation Overview: Heritable Changes in Gene Expression Without Alteration in DNA Sequence
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of epigenetic mechanisms, specifically DNA methylation and histone modification, and their role in regulating gene expression, particularly in the context of genomic imprinting and disease development. (考察表观遗传机制，特别是DNA甲基化和组蛋白修饰，及其在调控基因表达中的作用，特别是在基因组印记和疾病发展中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness and wasting, primarily affecting his proximal limbs and facial muscles. He also reports difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) and slurred speech (dysarthria). His past medical history is unremarkable, but his father died at age 55 from a similar neuromuscular disorder. On physical examination, the patient exhibits ptosis, facial muscle weakness (giving a "myopathic facies"), and proximal muscle weakness (4/5 strength in hip flexors and shoulder abductors). Reflexes are normal. Electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy reveals type II fiber atrophy and increased connective tissue. Genetic testing for common muscular dystrophies (e.g., Duchenne, Becker, Limb-Girdle) is negative. However, methylation analysis of the *MYOT* gene promoter region reveals hypermethylation in the patient's muscle tissue, while his unaffected mother shows normal methylation patterns. His father's tissue was unavailable for testing. The *MYOT* gene encodes a protein involved in muscle development and maintenance. The patient's unaffected mother carries one normal allele and one allele with a known mutation in the *MYOT* gene that predisposes to hypermethylation of the promoter region in offspring inheriting that allele.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for the patient's phenotype?

(A) A missense mutation in the *MYOT* gene leading to a non-functional protein, inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern.
(B) Genomic imprinting of the *MYOT* gene, where the mutated allele inherited from the mother is silenced due to methylation, leading to haploinsufficiency.
(C) A trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *MYOT* gene promoter, causing transcriptional silencing.
(D) A chromosomal translocation involving the *MYOT* gene, resulting in its overexpression.
(E) A frameshift mutation in the *MYOT* gene, leading to premature termination of translation and a truncated protein.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with progressive proximal muscle weakness, dysphagia, dysarthria, ptosis, myopathic facies. This clinical picture is suggestive of a late-onset myopathy or muscular dystrophy.
*   **Family History:** Father died young (55) with a similar disorder, suggesting a genetic component. Mother is unaffected.
*   **Physical Exam:** Findings consistent with a myopathy (proximal weakness, ptosis, facial weakness).
*   **Diagnostic Tests:**
    *   EMG: Myopathic changes confirm muscle origin.
    *   Muscle Biopsy: Type II fiber atrophy and increased connective tissue are common in muscular dystrophies.
    *   Genetic Testing (Standard): Negative for common muscular dystrophies, ruling out typical mutations in known genes.
    *   *MYOT* Gene Promoter Methylation Analysis: Hypermethylation in the patient's muscle tissue. Normal methylation in the unaffected mother.
*   **Additional Information:** The mother carries a mutation in the *MYOT* gene that predisposes to hypermethylation of the promoter region in offspring inheriting that allele.
*   **Deduction:** The patient has a myopathy linked to the *MYOT* gene. Standard genetic tests are negative, but methylation analysis shows hypermethylation of the *MYOT* promoter in the patient's muscle. The mother carries a mutation that predisposes to this hypermethylation. The father had a similar condition. This points towards an epigenetic mechanism, specifically DNA methylation, silencing the *MYOT* gene. The fact that the mother is unaffected but carries the predisposing mutation, and the father was affected, suggests a potential role for genomic imprinting or parent-of-origin effects.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Epigenetics:** Refers to heritable changes in gene expression that occur without alterations to the underlying DNA sequence. Key mechanisms include DNA methylation (typically cytosine methylation at CpG islands, often leading to gene silencing) and histone modifications (acetylation, methylation, phosphorylation, ubiquitination, etc., which alter chromatin structure and accessibility).
*   **DNA Methylation:** The addition of a methyl group to cytosine bases, primarily at CpG dinucleotides. Hypermethylation of promoter regions is strongly associated with transcriptional repression. Enzymes involved include DNA methyltransferases (DNMTs).
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This is established during gametogenesis through differential DNA methylation. One allele (either maternal or paternal) is silenced, while the other is expressed.
*   **The Case:** The patient's *MYOT* gene promoter is hypermethylated, leading to gene silencing and likely causing the myopathy. The mother carries a mutation that predisposes the *MYOT* allele she transmits to hypermethylation. The fact that the mother is unaffected suggests that the *MYOT* gene might be subject to imprinting, or that the mutation itself doesn't cause disease but only predisposes to silencing via methylation. Given the father's similar condition and the mother's unaffected status despite carrying the predisposing mutation, the most plausible explanation is that the *MYOT* gene is imprinted, and the mutated allele inherited from the mother is silenced by methylation. This leads to haploinsufficiency (having only one functional copy of the gene), causing the disease phenotype. The father likely inherited a non-methylated, mutated allele from his mother (or a different mutation), leading to disease. The mother's allele, even if mutated, is silenced by methylation in her own somatic cells (explaining her lack of symptoms) and potentially in the offspring inheriting it (explaining the patient's phenotype).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) A missense mutation in the *MYOT* gene leading to a non-functional protein, inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern.** This is incorrect. While a missense mutation *could* cause a myopathy, the key finding here is promoter hypermethylation, not a coding sequence mutation. Furthermore, the negative standard genetic testing and the specific methylation findings point away from a simple missense mutation. The inheritance pattern is also not clearly autosomal dominant, as the mother is unaffected.
*   **(C) A trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *MYOT* gene promoter, causing transcriptional silencing.** This is incorrect. Trinucleotide repeat expansions are a known cause of some genetic disorders (e.g., Huntington's disease, myotonic dystrophy), but the vignette specifically mentions *hypermethylation* of the promoter, not a repeat expansion. While repeat expansions can sometimes influence methylation patterns, the primary mechanism described is methylation itself.
*   **(D) A chromosomal translocation involving the *MYOT* gene, resulting in its overexpression.** This is incorrect. Translocations can lead to gene dysregulation, but they typically cause overexpression (if a gene is placed near a strong enhancer) or loss of function (if a gene is disrupted or moved to a repressive region). The vignette describes gene *silencing* due to hypermethylation, not overexpression. Also, translocations are usually detectable by karyotyping or FISH, which were not mentioned as being performed or positive.
*   **(E) A frameshift mutation in the *MYOT* gene, leading to premature termination of translation and a truncated protein.** This is incorrect. Similar to option A, a frameshift mutation is a type of coding sequence mutation. The key finding is promoter hypermethylation, which affects transcription initiation, not translation. Standard genetic testing was negative, making a frameshift mutation less likely as the primary cause, although it could be present in the mother's allele. However, the *mechanism* causing the patient's phenotype is the methylation-induced silencing.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation and histone modifications, are crucial regulators of gene expression and can be heritable. Genomic imprinting is a specific type of epigenetic regulation where gene expression depends on the parent of origin. Aberrant epigenetic modifications, like promoter hypermethylation, can lead to gene silencing and disease, even in the absence of DNA sequence mutations. This case highlights how epigenetic mechanisms can contribute to complex genetic disorders and explains why standard genetic sequencing might be negative. Key differentials for late-onset myopathies include various muscular dystrophies (Duchenne, Becker, Limb-Girdle, Facioscapulohumeral), metabolic myopathies, and inflammatory myopathies. This case specifically points to an epigenetic mechanism involving *MYOT* gene silencing.

<a id='question-052'></a>

### Question 052: DNA Methylation Dynamics: CpG Methylation at Promoter Regions Mediating Long-Term Gene Silencing
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: DNA methylation, specifically CpG methylation at promoter regions, is an epigenetic modification that leads to long-term gene silencing by inhibiting transcription factor binding and recruiting methyl-binding domain proteins, which in turn recruit histone deacetylases (HDACs) and chromatin remodeling complexes, leading to chromatin condensation and transcriptional repression. (DNA甲基化，特别是启动子区域的CpG甲基化，是一种表观遗传修饰，通过抑制转录因子结合和募集甲基结合结构域蛋白来导致长期基因沉默，后者又募集组蛋白去乙酰化酶（HDACs）和染色质重塑复合物，导致染色质凝结和转录抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive fatigue, shortness of breath, and palpitations over the past 6 months. His past medical history is significant for smoking 2 packs of cigarettes per day for 40 years. Physical examination reveals pallor, tachycardia (110 bpm), and a grade II/VI systolic murmur. Complete blood count (CBC) shows hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (normal: 13.5-17.5 g/dL), mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 70 fL (normal: 80-100 fL), and platelet count of 450,000/µL (normal: 150,000-450,000/µL). Peripheral blood smear shows microcytic, hypochromic red blood cells. Serum iron studies reveal low serum iron (40 µg/dL, normal: 60-170 µg/dL), low total iron-binding capacity (TIBC) (150 µg/dL, normal: 240-450 µg/dL), and high serum ferritin (1000 ng/mL, normal: 20-300 ng/mL). Bone marrow biopsy shows erythroid hyperplasia with ring sideroblasts. Genetic testing reveals a somatic mutation in the *SF3B1* gene in the patient's bone marrow cells. The patient is diagnosed with myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS). Research indicates that mutations in *SF3B1*, a component of the spliceosome, are frequently associated with MDS and often lead to aberrant splicing of genes involved in epigenetic regulation, including those encoding DNA methyltransferases (DNMTs). Specifically, mutations in *SF3B1* can lead to altered expression of DNMT3A, resulting in global hypomethylation and promoter-specific hypermethylation, contributing to the malignant transformation. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains how aberrant splicing of *SF3B1* leading to altered DNMT3A expression contributes to the pathogenesis of MDS in this patient?

(A) Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to increased acetylation of histones, promoting gene transcription and cellular proliferation.
(B) Increased expression of DNMT3A leads to decreased acetylation of histones, inhibiting gene transcription and promoting cellular differentiation.
(C) Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to decreased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, promoting gene transcription and cellular proliferation.
(D) Increased expression of DNMT3A leads to increased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, inhibiting gene transcription and promoting cellular differentiation.
(E) Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to increased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, inhibiting gene transcription and promoting cellular differentiation.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male smoker with fatigue, shortness of breath, palpitations, pallor, tachycardia, and systolic murmur, suggestive of anemia.
*   **Lab Findings:** Microcytic, hypochromic anemia (low Hb, low MCV), thrombocytosis (high platelets), low serum iron, low TIBC, high ferritin, and ring sideroblasts in bone marrow. These findings are characteristic of myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS), specifically MDS with ring sideroblasts (MDS-RS).
*   **Genetic Finding:** Somatic mutation in *SF3B1* gene in bone marrow cells. *SF3B1* mutations are common in MDS, particularly MDS-RS.
*   **Pathophysiological Link:** *SF3B1* mutations lead to aberrant splicing, affecting genes involved in epigenetic regulation, including *DNMT3A*. This results in altered DNMT3A expression (often increased, but can be decreased depending on the specific splice variant).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which altered DNMT3A expression (due to *SF3B1* mutation) contributes to MDS pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **DNA Methylation:** DNA methylation is a crucial epigenetic modification, primarily occurring at cytosine bases within CpG dinucleotides (cytosine followed by guanine).
*   **CpG Islands:** CpG islands are regions of the genome with a high frequency of CpG sites, often located in or near gene promoter regions.
*   **DNMTs:** DNA methyltransferases (DNMTs) are enzymes responsible for catalyzing the addition of a methyl group to cytosine bases. In mammals, the main maintenance methyltransferases are DNMT1, DNMT3A, and DNMT3B. DNMT3A and DNMT3B are *de novo* methyltransferases, establishing new methylation patterns, while DNMT1 primarily maintains existing methylation patterns during DNA replication.
*   **Promoter Methylation & Gene Silencing:** Methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions is generally associated with transcriptional repression (gene silencing). Methylation can directly inhibit the binding of transcription factors to the promoter. Furthermore, methylated DNA can recruit methyl-binding domain (MBD) proteins, which in turn recruit histone deacetylases (HDACs) and chromatin remodeling complexes. HDACs remove acetyl groups from histones, leading to chromatin condensation (heterochromatin formation) and reduced gene transcription.
*   **SF3B1 & MDS:** *SF3B1* encodes a core component of the spliceosome, the machinery responsible for RNA splicing. Mutations in *SF3B1* lead to aberrant splicing of various pre-mRNAs, including those encoding epigenetic regulators like DNMT3A.
*   **DNMT3A in MDS:** Aberrant splicing of *SF3B1* often leads to altered expression levels of DNMT3A. While the effect can vary (increase or decrease), increased expression of DNMT3A is frequently observed in MDS. Increased DNMT3A activity leads to increased methylation of CpG islands, particularly in promoter regions of tumor suppressor genes or genes involved in normal hematopoiesis.
*   **Pathogenesis in MDS:** Increased methylation of promoter regions by elevated DNMT3A activity silences genes critical for normal blood cell development and differentiation. This silencing contributes to the ineffective hematopoiesis, cytopenias, and increased risk of transformation to acute myeloid leukemia (AML) characteristic of MDS. The silencing of differentiation genes promotes a progenitor cell state, contributing to the dysplastic features.

##### 3. Linking to the Options
*   The vignette states *SF3B1* mutation leads to altered *DNMT3A* expression. The question asks how this altered expression contributes to MDS.
*   Option (D) states increased DNMT3A leads to increased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, inhibiting transcription and promoting cellular differentiation. This aligns with the known role of DNMT3A in establishing methylation patterns and the consequence of promoter methylation (gene silencing). While MDS is characterized by *impaired* differentiation, the silencing of genes *promoting* differentiation by hypermethylation contributes to the overall dysplastic phenotype. The option correctly links increased DNMT3A to increased methylation and transcriptional inhibition.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to increased acetylation of histones, promoting gene transcription and cellular proliferation. This is incorrect. Decreased DNMT3A would lead to *decreased* methylation (hypomethylation), not increased acetylation. Hypomethylation can lead to increased transcription, but the primary effect of DNMT3A is methylation. Also, while proliferation is involved in MDS, the core mechanism described here is incorrect regarding DNMT3A activity and histone acetylation. Decreased DNMT3A activity leads to *less* methylation, which can *activate* genes, but the question stem implies altered DNMT3A expression *due to SF3B1 mutation*, which often leads to *increased* DNMT3A activity or altered specificity, resulting in hypermethylation.
- (B): Increased expression of DNMT3A leads to decreased acetylation of histones, inhibiting gene transcription and promoting cellular differentiation. This is partially correct in that increased DNMT3A leads to increased methylation and inhibits transcription. However, methylation primarily recruits MBD proteins and HDACs, leading to histone *deacetylation* and chromatin condensation, not decreased acetylation directly by DNMT3A. Furthermore, while silencing certain genes might seem like promoting differentiation (by removing brakes), the overall effect in MDS is impaired differentiation due to silencing of key hematopoietic genes. The primary mechanism is methylation, not histone acetylation changes directly caused by DNMT3A.
- (C): Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to decreased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, promoting gene transcription and cellular proliferation. This is incorrect. Decreased DNMT3A would lead to *decreased* methylation (hypomethylation), which *can* promote transcription. However, the *SF3B1* mutations often lead to *increased* or altered (e.g., misdirected) DNMT3A activity, resulting in hypermethylation, not hypomethylation. Hypomethylation is also seen in cancer but is not the primary mechanism linked to *increased* DNMT3A activity.
- (E): Decreased expression of DNMT3A leads to increased methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions, inhibiting gene transcription and promoting cellular differentiation. This is contradictory. Decreased DNMT3A expression would lead to *decreased* methylation, not increased methylation. The rest of the statement follows logically from increased methylation (inhibition of transcription), but the premise is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DNA methylation, particularly at CpG islands in promoter regions, is a key epigenetic mechanism for long-term gene silencing. Increased activity of DNA methyltransferases (DNMTs), such as DNMT3A, leads to hypermethylation, which inhibits transcription factor binding and recruits chromatin remodeling complexes, resulting in transcriptional repression. Aberrant splicing caused by mutations in genes like *SF3B1* can alter the expression or function of epigenetic regulators like DNMT3A, contributing to diseases like MDS. Key differentials for MDS include other myeloproliferative neoplasms and acute leukemias. Understanding the role of epigenetic modifications is crucial for comprehending cancer pathogenesis.

### Question 053: Rett Syndrome and MeCP2 Function
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rett syndrome is caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene, which encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2). MeCP2 binds to methylated CpG islands in DNA, primarily in neuronal cells, and recruits transcriptional corepressor complexes, such as histone deacetylases (HDACs), leading to chromatin condensation and gene silencing. Loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2* disrupt this process, resulting in abnormal brain development and neurological dysfunction. (Rett 综合征由 *MECP2* 基因突变引起，该基因编码甲基 CpG 结合蛋白 2 (MeCP2)。MeCP2 结合 DNA 中甲基化的 CpG 岛，主要在神经元中，并募集转录核心抑制复合物，如组蛋白去乙酰化酶 (HDAC)，导致染色质凝缩和基因沉默。*MECP2* 基因失功能的突变会破坏这一过程，导致异常的脑部发育和神经功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 15-month-old female presents to her pediatrician with a history of normal development for the first 6-12 months, followed by a period of regression. Her parents report loss of purposeful hand skills (e.g., no longer waves bye-bye or reaches for objects), decreased verbal communication, and the development of stereotypic hand movements such as hand-wringing or clapping. Physical examination reveals microcephaly (head circumference 2 standard deviations below the mean for age), hypotonia, and ataxia. Neurological examination shows brisk deep tendon reflexes and clonus. A chromosomal microarray analysis is normal. Whole-exome sequencing reveals a novel heterozygous missense mutation in the *MECP2* gene located on the X chromosome. Biochemical analysis of fibroblasts from the patient shows decreased binding of MeCP2 protein to methylated DNA compared to controls.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the pathophysiology underlying this patient's condition?

(A) The mutation leads to increased acetylation of histones at gene promoters, resulting in overexpression of genes critical for neuronal development and function.
(B) The mutation results in a gain-of-function effect, where the altered MeCP2 protein binds to unmethylated DNA, leading to inappropriate transcriptional activation.
(C) The mutation causes a loss-of-function of MeCP2, impairing its ability to bind methylated DNA and recruit transcriptional corepressor complexes, leading to aberrant gene expression patterns in neurons.
(D) The mutation leads to the production of a truncated MeCP2 protein that sequesters wild-type MeCP2 protein, forming non-functional aggregates and causing a dominant-negative effect.
(E) The mutation results in increased methylation of the *MECP2* gene itself, leading to epigenetic silencing of the wild-type allele and reduced MeCP2 protein levels.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 15-month-old female with initial normal development followed by regression (loss of hand skills, decreased verbal communication), stereotypic hand movements, microcephaly, hypotonia, ataxia, brisk reflexes, clonus. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of Rett syndrome.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous missense mutation in the *MECP2* gene on the X chromosome. *MECP2* mutations are the primary cause of Rett syndrome.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Decreased binding of MeCP2 protein to methylated DNA in fibroblasts. This indicates a functional defect in the MeCP2 protein's ability to interact with its target (methylated DNA).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathophysiology.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Rett syndrome is primarily caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene, located on the X chromosome (Xq28).
- The *MECP2* gene encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2).
- MeCP2 is a protein that belongs to the methyl-CpG-binding domain (MBD) protein family.
- The primary function of MeCP2 is to bind specifically to methylated cytosine residues within CpG dinucleotides in DNA. CpG islands are regions of DNA with a high frequency of CpG sites, often found near gene promoters.
- DNA methylation, particularly at CpG islands, is an epigenetic modification associated with gene silencing.
- Once bound to methylated DNA, MeCP2 recruits various protein complexes, including transcriptional corepressors like histone deacetylases (HDACs), histone methyltransferases (e.g., SETDB1), and chromatin remodeling complexes (e.g., NuRD complex).
- The recruitment of these corepressor complexes leads to modifications of histones (e.g., deacetylation, methylation) and chromatin compaction, resulting in transcriptional repression of the associated genes.
- MeCP2 is predominantly expressed in neurons and plays a crucial role in neuronal development, maturation, and synaptic plasticity.
- Loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2* (most common cause of Rett syndrome) disrupt the normal function of MeCP2. This leads to impaired binding to methylated DNA and/or defective recruitment of corepressor complexes.
- The consequence is aberrant gene expression patterns in the brain, with the upregulation of genes that should normally be silenced and the downregulation of genes that should normally be active. This dysregulation of gene expression is thought to underlie the neurological symptoms of Rett syndrome.
- The vignette states a missense mutation leading to decreased binding of MeCP2 to methylated DNA. This directly points to a loss-of-function mechanism where the protein cannot perform its primary role of binding methylated DNA and recruiting corepressors.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The mutation leads to *decreased* binding of MeCP2 to methylated DNA, impairing its function as a corepressor. Increased histone acetylation is associated with gene *activation*, not the silencing effect mediated by MeCP2. MeCP2 recruits HDACs, which *remove* acetyl groups, leading to chromatin condensation and gene repression.
- (B): Incorrect. The vignette describes a *loss* of function (decreased binding to methylated DNA), not a gain-of-function. Furthermore, MeCP2 specifically binds *methylated* DNA; binding to unmethylated DNA would be aberrant and not the typical mechanism. Gain-of-function mutations in *MECP2* are rare and typically associated with different phenotypes (e.g., MECP2 duplication syndrome).
- (D): Incorrect. While some mutations can lead to truncated proteins or dominant-negative effects, the vignette specifically describes a missense mutation and provides biochemical evidence of decreased binding affinity, suggesting a loss-of-function mechanism rather than sequestration of wild-type protein. The primary mechanism in most Rett syndrome cases is loss of function.
- (E): Incorrect. The vignette describes a heterozygous missense mutation in the *MECP2* gene itself, not increased methylation of the gene. While epigenetic silencing can occur, the primary defect in Rett syndrome is the mutation in the *MECP2* gene leading to a dysfunctional protein, not silencing of the wild-type allele by methylation of the *MECP2* locus itself.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- High-Yield Takeaway: Rett syndrome is caused by mutations in *MECP2*, which encodes a protein that binds methylated DNA and recruits corepressor complexes (like HDACs) to silence gene expression. Loss-of-function mutations disrupt this process, leading to neurological dysfunction.
- Differentials: Other genetic syndromes with neurological regression (e.g., Angelman syndrome, Canavan disease, mitochondrial disorders), but Rett syndrome is specifically linked to *MECP2* mutations and characterized by the specific clinical features described (loss of hand skills, stereotypic movements, microcephaly) and the molecular mechanism of MeCP2 dysfunction.

<a id='question-054'></a>

### Question 054: Rett Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rett syndrome is an X-linked dominant neurodevelopmental disorder primarily affecting females, caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene, leading to developmental regression, stereotypic hand movements, gait abnormalities, seizures, and breathing irregularities. (Rett 综合征是一种 X 连锁显性神经发育障碍，主要影响女性，由 *MECP2* 基因突变引起，导致发育倒退、刻板手部动作、步态异常、癫痫发作和呼吸不规则。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 14-month-old female presents to the pediatrician with a history of normal development for the first 6-9 months of life, followed by a period of developmental regression. Her parents report a loss of purposeful hand skills, replaced by stereotypic hand-wringing and clapping movements. She now exhibits gait ataxia, frequent episodes of hyperventilation followed by breath-holding (apnea), and has recently started having generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Physical examination reveals microcephaly (head circumference below the 3rd percentile) and spasticity in the lower extremities. Her mother reports no significant medical or family history, although she mentions a maternal uncle who died in infancy due to an unexplained neurological condition. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Molecular genetic testing is performed. Which of the following molecular findings is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) A deletion of the *SMN1* gene on chromosome 5q13, leading to reduced survival motor neuron protein production.
(B) A missense mutation in the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome, resulting in a fragile X premutation with an expanded CGG repeat number.
(C) A mutation in the *MECP2* gene on the X chromosome, leading to a non-functional methyl-CpG-binding protein 2.
(D) A duplication of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, resulting in maternal uniparental disomy and loss of paternal UBE3A expression.
(E) A point mutation in the *HTT* gene on chromosome 4p16.3, causing an expanded CAG repeat number and production of mutant huntingtin protein.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 14-month-old female. Rett syndrome predominantly affects females.
*   **Developmental History:** Normal development for 6-9 months, followed by regression. This is a hallmark feature of Rett syndrome.
*   **Neurological Symptoms:** Loss of purposeful hand skills, stereotypic hand movements (wringing, clapping), gait ataxia, seizures, breathing irregularities (hyperventilation, apnea). These are classic signs of Rett syndrome.
*   **Physical Findings:** Microcephaly, spasticity. Common findings in Rett syndrome.
*   **Family History:** Maternal uncle died in infancy with neurological issues. This suggests a possible X-linked inheritance pattern, as the mother is a carrier and could have passed the mutation to her daughter. Males with Rett syndrome mutations typically have severe neonatal encephalopathy and do not survive infancy.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX. Rules out chromosomal aneuploidies like Down syndrome or Turner syndrome.
*   **Molecular Testing:** Implied to be the diagnostic test for a suspected genetic disorder like Rett syndrome.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Rett syndrome is caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene located on the X chromosome (Xq28). This gene encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2). MeCP2 is a protein that binds to methylated DNA, primarily at CpG islands, and recruits other proteins to regulate gene expression, particularly in neurons. It acts as a transcriptional repressor for some genes and an activator for others, playing a crucial role in neuronal maturation, synapse formation, and plasticity. Mutations in *MECP2* are typically *de novo* (spontaneous) in about 99% of cases, explaining the lack of family history in many instances, although X-linked inheritance is possible if the mother is a carrier (often with milder symptoms or somatic mosaicism). The loss of functional MeCP2 protein leads to widespread transcriptional dysregulation in the brain, resulting in the characteristic neurodevelopmental regression and clinical features observed in Rett syndrome. The disease follows an X-linked dominant inheritance pattern, but is almost exclusively seen in females because mutations are typically lethal in males *in utero* or shortly after birth, leading to severe neonatal encephalopathy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA)**. This is caused by deletions or mutations in the *SMN1* gene, leading to deficiency of the survival motor neuron (SMN) protein. SMA presents with progressive muscle weakness and atrophy, typically starting in infancy or early childhood. It does not involve the developmental regression, stereotypic hand movements, or microcephaly characteristic of Rett syndrome. The genetic mechanism is autosomal recessive.
- (B): **Fragile X Syndrome (Premutation)**. This is caused by an expansion of a CGG repeat in the 5' untranslated region of the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome. While fragile X syndrome is X-linked and can cause intellectual disability and developmental delay, the premutation range (55-200 repeats) is typically associated with Fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome (FXTAS) or Fragile X-associated primary ovarian insufficiency (FXPOI) in females, not the classic Rett syndrome phenotype. Full mutations (>200 repeats) cause Fragile X syndrome, characterized by intellectual disability, distinct facial features, and macroorchidism in males. Rett syndrome involves developmental regression after a period of normal development, which is not typical for Fragile X syndrome.
- (D): **Angelman Syndrome**. This is typically caused by a deletion or inactivation of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13. Angelman syndrome presents with severe developmental delay, intellectual disability, ataxia, seizures, characteristic happy demeanor with frequent laughter, and microcephaly. While there are some overlapping features like ataxia, seizures, and microcephaly, the developmental regression pattern, stereotypic hand movements, and breathing abnormalities are more characteristic of Rett syndrome. Angelman syndrome is caused by a defect in the maternal copy of *UBE3A* due to genomic imprinting.
- (E): **Huntington Disease**. This is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by an expansion of a CAG repeat in the *HTT* gene. Huntington disease typically presents in adulthood (30s-40s) with chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms. Juvenile Huntington disease can occur but presents differently and is not associated with the specific features of Rett syndrome like developmental regression after normal development, stereotypic hand movements, or microcephaly in infancy.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rett syndrome is a classic X-linked dominant disorder caused by *MECP2* mutations, characterized by developmental regression after 6-18 months, loss of purposeful hand skills, stereotypic hand movements, microcephaly, ataxia, seizures, and breathing abnormalities, primarily affecting females. Key differentials include other neurodevelopmental disorders like Angelman syndrome, Fragile X syndrome, and Spinal Muscular Atrophy, which have distinct genetic causes and clinical presentations. Remember the specific pattern of regression and hand stereotypies for Rett syndrome.

<a id='question-055'></a>

### Question 055: Genomic Imprinting & Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting leads to monoallelic gene expression based on the parent of origin, resulting in specific syndromes when imprinted regions are deleted, duplicated, or undergo uniparental disomy. (基因组印记导致基于亲本来源的单等位基因表达，当印记区域被缺失、重复或发生单亲二倍体时，会导致特定的综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating (hyperphagia) starting around age 2. He was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy. His birth weight was normal, but he has gained excessive weight since infancy. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, hypogonadism (small testes), and characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. His parents are of average height and have no history of similar symptoms. Karyotype analysis shows a normal 46,XY. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 reveals that the paternal allele is absent in the patient's cells.

(A) The patient's symptoms are most likely due to a mutation in the maternal allele of the *UBE3A* gene, which is subject to genomic imprinting.
(B) The patient's symptoms are most likely due to a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of function of imprinted genes on the maternal allele.
(C) The patient's symptoms are most likely due to a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of function of imprinted genes normally expressed from the paternal allele.
(D) The patient's symptoms are most likely due to an increased number of copies of the maternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region, leading to overexpression of imprinted genes normally silenced on the maternal allele.
(E) The patient's symptoms are most likely due to uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, resulting in trisomy 21 (Down syndrome).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia (excessive eating), short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism, characteristic facial features. This constellation of symptoms strongly suggests Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
*   **Family History:** Parents are unaffected, suggesting a *de novo* event or a condition not typically inherited in a simple Mendelian pattern.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidy like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or Klinefelter syndrome (XXY).
*   **FISH Analysis:** Absence of the paternal allele for the *SNRPN* gene locus (15q11-q13) is the critical finding. The *SNRPN* gene is located within the imprinted region on chromosome 15.
*   **Deduction:** PWS is a classic example of a genomic imprinting disorder. The symptoms described are characteristic of PWS. The FISH result indicates a lack of paternal contribution from the critical region on chromosome 15. This points to a mechanism involving the loss or silencing of paternally expressed genes in this region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** A phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This differential expression is established during gametogenesis through epigenetic modifications (DNA methylation, histone modification) that silence one allele (either maternal or paternal).
*   **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) Locus:** Located on chromosome 15q11-q13. This region contains several genes, many of which are imprinted.
*   **Paternal Imprinting:** In the PWS critical region, the paternal allele is normally expressed, while the maternal allele is silenced (imprinted). Key genes expressed from the paternal allele include *SNRPN*, *NDN*, *SNORD116*, and *SNORD115*.
*   **Mechanism of PWS:** PWS typically results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. This can occur through:
    *   **Paternal Deletion:** The most common cause (~70%), involving deletion of the paternal copy of the 15q11-q13 region.
    *   **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal allele in this region is normally imprinted (silenced), the individual lacks functional paternal copies of the imprinted genes.
    *   **Imprinting Defect:** A rare cause where the paternal allele is incorrectly imprinted (silenced) like the maternal allele.
*   **FISH Result Interpretation:** The FISH result showing the absence of the paternal *SNRPN* allele directly indicates a loss of the paternal contribution to this critical region. This is consistent with a paternal deletion or maternal UPD. Both mechanisms lead to the same functional outcome: lack of expression of paternally expressed genes required for normal development.
*   **Connecting to the Options:** The patient's clinical picture (PWS) and the FISH result (absent paternal 15q11-q13 allele) point directly to the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in this region. Option (C) accurately describes this scenario.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): *UBE3A* is located on chromosome 15q11.2, within the PWS/Angelman syndrome critical region. However, *UBE3A* is subject to *maternal* imprinting – the maternal allele is expressed in the brain, while the paternal allele is silenced. Loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A* allele causes Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and a happy demeanor, which is distinct from the PWS phenotype. The vignette describes PWS symptoms and loss of the *paternal* allele, making this incorrect.
- (B): This option describes a deletion of the *maternal* chromosome 15q11-q13 region. Loss of the maternal allele would lead to Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS, because the key genes for AS (*UBE3A*) are expressed only from the maternal allele in the brain. The vignette describes PWS symptoms and loss of the *paternal* allele, making this incorrect.
- (D): This option describes an increased number of copies (duplication) of the *maternal* chromosome 15q11-q13 region. This would lead to overexpression of genes normally silenced on the maternal allele, potentially causing a different syndrome (e.g., related to overexpression of *MEST* or *PLAG1*), but not PWS. PWS is caused by the *loss* of function of paternally expressed genes. The vignette describes loss of the *paternal* allele, making this incorrect.
- (E): This option describes uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). Down syndrome has a distinct phenotype (characteristic facial features, intellectual disability, congenital heart defects, etc.) and is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 21, not a defect in the imprinted region of chromosome 15. The vignette describes PWS symptoms and a specific genetic finding on chromosome 15, making this incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting causes parent-of-origin specific gene expression. Prader-Willi syndrome results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region (e.g., via paternal deletion or maternal UPD). Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region. Key differential: PWS involves hyperphagia and hypotonia, while AS involves ataxia, seizures, and happy demeanor. Always consider imprinting disorders when evaluating developmental delay and characteristic phenotypes.

<a id='question-056'></a>

### Question 056: Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy leading to specific genetic disorders; understanding the molecular basis of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) involving the 15q11-q13 region, specifically maternal deletion or paternal UPD, and the role of imprinting in gene silencing. (基因组印记和单亲二倍体导致特定遗传性疾病；理解Prader-Willi综合征 (PWS) 的分子基础，涉及 15q11-q13 区域，具体为母系缺失或父系单亲二倍体，以及印记在基因沉默中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his development and feeding habits. Since infancy, he has exhibited hypotonia (poor muscle tone) and feeding difficulties, requiring specialized formula and frequent feeding. Over the past year, his parents have noticed a significant increase in his appetite, leading to hyperphagia (excessive eating) and rapid weight gain, particularly of fat mass. He also displays developmental delay, with limited speech and cognitive impairment. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, and hypogonadism (small testes). His parents report no significant family history of similar conditions, but his maternal grandfather had a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus. Karyotyping reveals a normal male chromosome complement (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the paternal chromosome 15 is missing the region normally inherited from the father, while the maternal chromosome 15 appears normal.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) A deletion of the paternal copy of the *SNRPN* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of function of this imprinted gene.
(B) A deletion of the maternal copy of the *SNRPN* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of function of this imprinted gene.
(C) A deletion of the paternal copy of the *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of function of this imprinted gene.
(D) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, resulting in the inheritance of two maternal copies of the 15q11-q13 region and loss of expression of paternally expressed genes in this region.
(E) A translocation involving chromosome 15 and another autosome, disrupting the *SNRPN* gene on the maternal chromosome.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Family History:** Maternal grandfather with type 2 diabetes. While not directly causative, diabetes can be associated with PWS later in life.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY, ruling out aneuploidy.
- **FISH Analysis:** Shows the paternal chromosome 15 is missing the 15q11-q13 region. This is a critical finding. The maternal chromosome 15 is normal.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the condition.

The clinical features strongly point towards PWS. The FISH result indicates a deletion of the paternal contribution to the 15q11-q13 region. PWS is caused by the lack of expression of paternally expressed genes in this region. The key genes involved are *SNRPN* and *NKX2-5*, which are normally expressed only from the paternal allele due to genomic imprinting. The maternal allele in this region is silenced (imprinted).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) is a complex genetic disorder caused by the loss of function of specific genes located in the 15q11-q13 region on the paternal chromosome 15. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, a phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, several genes are normally expressed *only* from the paternal allele, while others are expressed *only* from the maternal allele.

The critical paternally expressed genes implicated in PWS are *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N) and *NKX2-5* (NK2 Homeobox 5). These genes are essential for normal development, particularly in the brain and hypothalamus, which explains the neurological and metabolic features of PWS (hypotonia, developmental delay, hyperphagia, obesity).

The maternal allele of *SNRPN* and *NKX2-5* in the 15q11-q13 region is silenced through DNA methylation and histone modifications, preventing its expression. This silencing is established during gametogenesis.

The most common cause of PWS (~70% of cases) is a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. This deletion removes the paternally expressed *SNRPN* and *NKX2-5* genes, leading to the loss of their function.

Other causes include:
- Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 (~25% of cases): The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal copies of the genes in the 15q11-q13 region are imprinted (silenced), the individual lacks expression of the paternally expressed genes.
- Imprinting defects (~5% of cases): Errors in the imprinting process can lead to silencing of the paternal allele or activation of the maternal allele in the 15q11-q13 region.
- Small deletions or mutations within the paternal 15q11-q13 region.

In this specific case, the FISH analysis clearly shows a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. This deletion removes the paternal copy of the *SNRPN* gene (and *NKX2-5*), which is normally expressed. Since the maternal copy is imprinted and silenced, the patient lacks functional *SNRPN* protein, leading to PWS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the most common mechanism for PWS, which is a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, leading to the loss of function of paternally expressed genes like *SNRPN*. The FISH results in the vignette confirm this specific mechanism.
- (B): **Incorrect.** This option describes a deletion of the *maternal* copy of the *SNRPN* gene. This would not cause PWS because the *SNRPN* gene is paternally expressed in the 15q11-q13 region. Deletion of the maternal copy would not affect the expression of the paternally expressed *SNRPN*.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This option describes a deletion of the *paternal* copy of the *UBE3A* gene. While *UBE3A* is located in the 15q11-q13 region, its imprinting pattern is the opposite of *SNRPN*. *UBE3A* is normally expressed *only* from the *maternal* allele. Loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A* gene causes Angelman Syndrome, not PWS. Deletion of the paternal *UBE3A* would not cause Angelman Syndrome because the maternal copy is active.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This option describes maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15. While maternal UPD is a cause of PWS, the FISH results in the vignette specifically show a *deletion* of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, not UPD. UPD would mean the patient has two maternal chromosomes 15, but both would be intact, just both maternally derived and imprinted.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This option describes a translocation disrupting the *SNRPN* gene on the *maternal* chromosome. This would not cause PWS because *SNRPN* is paternally expressed. Furthermore, the vignette's FISH results point to a deletion of the paternal region, not a translocation affecting the maternal region.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi Syndrome is caused by the lack of expression of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region, most commonly due to a paternal deletion. Key features include hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, and hypogonadism. Remember the imprinting pattern: *SNRPN* and *NKX2-5* are paternally expressed, while *UBE3A* is maternally expressed. Loss of maternal *UBE3A* function causes Angelman Syndrome. Maternal UPD 15 or imprinting defects are less common causes of PWS. (Prader-Willi综合征是由15q11-q13区域父系基因表达缺失引起的，最常见原因是父系缺失。关键特征包括肌张力低下、贪食、肥胖、发育迟缓和性腺功能减退。记住印记模式：SNRPN和NKX2-5是父系表达的，而UBE3A是母系表达的。母系UBE3A功能丧失会导致Angelman综合征。母系单亲二倍体15或印记缺陷是PWS的不常见原因。)

<a id='question-057'></a>

### Question 057: Angelman Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy (UPD) leading to loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene, causing Angelman syndrome. (基因组印记和单亲二体畸 (UPD) 导致 *UBE3A* 基因功能丧失，引起 Angelman 综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-year-old girl presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, severe intellectual disability, ataxia, seizures (primarily tonic-clonic), and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter (often described as "happy puppet syndrome"). Her parents report that she rarely speaks and has difficulty walking. Her mother notes that the child's father has a history of epilepsy. Physical examination reveals microcephaly, jerky movements, and a characteristic wide-mouthed smile. A chromosomal microarray analysis (CMA) is performed, which is normal. However, methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region reveals a specific pattern. The mother's DNA shows normal methylation patterns for this region, while the father's DNA shows abnormal methylation patterns consistent with imprinting. The patient's DNA methylation analysis shows hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele, but the maternal allele is deleted.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the *SNRPN* gene within the 15q11-q13 region, leading to Prader-Willi syndrome.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, resulting in two copies of the paternally imprinted *UBE3A* gene and no active maternal copy.
(C) A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome that results in a non-functional protein, despite normal methylation.
(D) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, resulting in two copies of the paternally imprinted *UBE3A* gene and no active maternal copy.
(E) A translocation involving chromosome 21 and chromosome 15, disrupting the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 2-year-old girl with developmental delay, severe intellectual disability, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet syndrome"), limited speech, difficulty walking, microcephaly, jerky movements, wide-mouthed smile. These are classic features of Angelman syndrome.
- **Family History:** Father has epilepsy (can be associated with Angelman syndrome, though not specific).
- **Initial Test:** Chromosomal microarray (CMA) is normal, ruling out large deletions or duplications detectable by CMA.
- **Methylation Analysis:** This is the key diagnostic test for imprinting disorders.
    - Mother: Normal methylation pattern (expected).
    - Father: Abnormal methylation pattern consistent with imprinting (expected for males, who only have one X chromosome, so the 15q11-q13 region is not subject to X-inactivation and thus shows paternal imprinting).
    - Patient: Hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele (consistent with normal imprinting pattern). However, the maternal allele is *deleted*.
- **Deduction:** Angelman syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the *UBE3A* gene, which is normally expressed *only* from the maternal allele in the brain due to genomic imprinting. The paternal allele is silenced by methylation. The patient has a normal imprinting pattern (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation), but the maternal *UBE3A* allele is deleted. This means there is no functional maternal copy of *UBE3A*, and the paternal copy is silenced. Therefore, the patient has effectively lost the only source of functional *UBE3A* expression in the brain. This specific scenario (normal imprinting pattern + maternal deletion) is one of the known genetic causes of Angelman syndrome.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **UBE3A Gene:** Encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation. It is crucial for neuronal development and function.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, the *UBE3A* gene is imprinted, meaning it is expressed only from the maternal allele in the brain. The paternal allele is silenced by DNA methylation.
- **Angelman Syndrome Causes:** Loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A* allele can occur through several mechanisms:
    1.  **Maternal Deletion:** Deletion of the 15q11-q13 region encompassing the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome (most common cause, ~70%).
    2.  **Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. Both paternal copies have the *UBE3A* gene, but the paternal allele is imprinted (methylated and silenced) in the brain.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** A defect in the imprinting center leads to methylation of the maternal allele, silencing it, while the paternal allele remains silenced.
    4.  **UBE3A Mutation:** A mutation (e.g., nonsense, frameshift, splice site) in the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome that inactivates the protein.
- **Patient's Case:** The vignette explicitly states the maternal allele is deleted, and the methylation pattern is consistent with normal imprinting (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation). This confirms the loss of the maternal *UBE3A* contribution, leading to Angelman syndrome. The question asks for the *molecular mechanism*. The mechanism is the deletion of the maternal allele, leading to the absence of the normally expressed *UBE3A* gene. This deletion results in the patient having only the paternally inherited chromosome 15, which carries a silenced (*UBE3A*) allele in the brain. Therefore, the *effective* genetic state is equivalent to having two copies of the paternally imprinted chromosome 15, which is functionally the same as paternal UPD in terms of *UBE3A* expression. However, the *cause* is the deletion, not the UPD itself. Let's re-evaluate the options based on the *cause* described. The vignette states the maternal allele is deleted. This means the patient has one maternal chromosome 15 (which is deleted for *UBE3A*) and one paternal chromosome 15 (which has a silenced *UBE3A* allele). This situation is functionally equivalent to having two paternal copies, which occurs in paternal UPD. The question asks for the *mechanism*. The underlying cause is the maternal deletion. However, the *consequence* is the absence of a functional maternal *UBE3A* allele, leaving only the silenced paternal allele. This functional outcome is identical to paternal UPD. Option (D) describes this functional outcome. Let's reconsider the wording. The vignette states the maternal allele is deleted. This is the direct cause. However, none of the options state "maternal deletion". Option (D) describes the *genetic consequence* of having only the paternal chromosome 15 contributing the *UBE3A* gene (which is silenced). This is functionally equivalent to paternal UPD. Given the options, (D) describes the state of having only the paternally imprinted allele active (or rather, the only allele present is the paternally imprinted one), which is the functional defect. The maternal deletion *leads* to this state. Let's re-read the question: "Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?". The condition is Angelman syndrome due to loss of maternal *UBE3A*. The vignette states the maternal allele is deleted. This is the direct cause. However, option (D) describes the *result* of the deletion in terms of the chromosome origin of the *UBE3A* allele present. The patient effectively has two copies of the paternally imprinted chromosome 15. This is functionally identical to paternal UPD. Let's analyze the options again.
    - (A) Prader-Willi: Caused by loss of function of the *paternally* expressed *SNRPN* gene in the same region, usually due to paternal deletion or maternal UPD. Incorrect.
    - (B) Maternal UPD: Would result in two maternal copies of *UBE3A*, both normally expressed. Incorrect.
    - (C) *UBE3A* mutation: Possible cause, but the vignette specifies a deletion and normal methylation patterns, ruling this out as the *specific* mechanism described. Incorrect.
    - (D) Paternal UPD: Results in two copies of the paternally imprinted chromosome 15, meaning the *UBE3A* gene is silenced in the brain. This matches the functional consequence of the maternal deletion described in the vignette. Correct.
    - (E) Translocation: Not mentioned, and unlikely to cause this specific pattern. Incorrect.
    - **Conclusion:** Although the direct cause is maternal deletion, the functional consequence described (only the paternally imprinted allele present) is identical to paternal UPD. Given the options, (D) is the best fit describing the functional genetic state leading to the loss of maternal *UBE3A* expression.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Prader-Willi syndrome** is caused by the loss of function of genes in the same 15q11-q13 region, but specifically the *paternally* expressed *SNRPN* gene. This typically occurs due to a *paternal* deletion or *maternal* UPD. The clinical features (hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, hypogonadism) are distinct from Angelman syndrome. This option confuses the two imprinting disorders in this region and the parent-of-origin expression requirements.
- (B): **Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD)** of chromosome 15 would mean the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the *UBE3A* gene is normally expressed from the maternal allele, this would result in *two* functional copies of *UBE3A*, which is inconsistent with Angelman syndrome. This option confuses maternal UPD with paternal UPD.
- (C): A **mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome** is a possible cause of Angelman syndrome. However, the vignette specifically describes methylation analysis showing a normal imprinting pattern (maternal allele hypomethylated) but also states the maternal allele is *deleted*. A mutation would typically be associated with a hypomethylated maternal allele, but the deletion is the primary finding described. This option ignores the specific finding of maternal deletion.
- (E): A **translocation involving chromosome 21 and chromosome 15** is not a typical cause of Angelman syndrome. While translocations can disrupt genes, they don't usually lead to the specific imprinting pattern and loss of maternal *UBE3A* expression seen here. Furthermore, the vignette provides specific information about methylation analysis and deletion, making a translocation less likely and unsupported by the data.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman

<a id='question-058'></a>

### Question 058: Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome (BWS) is an overgrowth disorder caused by epigenetic dysregulation of imprinted genes on chromosome 11p15, leading to overexpression of IGF2 and/or loss of function of the paternally expressed H19 gene, resulting in characteristic clinical features and increased cancer risk. (贝克韦德曼综合征 (BWS) 是一种由 11p15 染色体上印记基因的表观遗传失调引起的过度生长疾病，导致 IGF2 过度表达和/或父本表达的 H19 基因功能丧失，从而产生特征性临床表现和增加患癌风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn male infant, the first child of healthy, non-consanguineous parents, is delivered at term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery. The infant weighs 5.2 kg (95th percentile) and measures 58 cm in length (98th percentile). Physical examination reveals macroglossia (tongue protruding from the mouth), an omphalocele containing non-rotated intestines, and bilateral ear creases. Abdominal ultrasound performed at 6 months of age shows a 7 cm left renal mass. Karyotype analysis is normal (46,XY). Methylation analysis of the 11p15 region reveals a loss of methylation at the paternally inherited allele for the H19 gene promoter and the IGF2 enhancer region.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this infant's clinical presentation?

(A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 11p15, resulting in biallelic expression of the H19 gene and silencing of the IGF2 gene.
(B) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 11p15, resulting in biallelic expression of the IGF2 gene and silencing of the H19 gene.
(C) A point mutation in the IGF2 gene leading to constitutive activation of the IGF2 receptor.
(D) A deletion of the maternal copy of the 11p15 region, resulting in monoallelic expression of the H19 gene and biallelic expression of the IGF2 gene.
(E) A translocation involving chromosome 11p15 and chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 11p15 and overexpression of both H19 and IGF2.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn male, large for gestational age (LGA):** Weight 5.2 kg (95th percentile), Length 58 cm (98th percentile). Suggests overgrowth syndrome.
- **Macroglossia, Omphalocele, Ear creases:** Classic triad of Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome (BWS).
- **Renal mass (Wilms tumor):** Increased risk of embryonal tumors, particularly Wilms tumor, is associated with BWS.
- **Normal karyotype (46,XY):** Rules out chromosomal aneuploidy as the primary cause.
- **Methylation analysis:** Loss of methylation at the paternal H19 promoter and IGF2 enhancer region. This is the key genetic finding. In normal development, the paternal allele of H19 is silenced by methylation, and the maternal allele is active. Conversely, the paternal allele of IGF2 is active, and the maternal allele is silenced by methylation. Loss of methylation on the paternal allele means the paternal H19 gene is *not* silenced, and the paternal IGF2 gene is *not* silenced.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Imprinting:** Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. The 11p15 region contains two key imprinted genes: IGF2 (Insulin-like Growth Factor 2) and H19 (a non-coding RNA gene).
- **Normal Imprinting at 11p15:**
    - Paternal allele: IGF2 is expressed; H19 is silenced (methylated promoter).
    - Maternal allele: IGF2 is silenced (methylated enhancer); H19 is expressed.
- **Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome (BWS) Pathogenesis:** BWS is caused by defects in imprinting control of the 11p15 region. The most common mechanism (~70% of cases) involves loss of methylation at the paternal IGF2 enhancer and/or H19 promoter.
- **Mechanism in this case:** The vignette states loss of methylation at the *paternal* H19 promoter and IGF2 enhancer. This means the paternal allele, which normally expresses IGF2 and silences H19, now expresses *both* IGF2 and H19. The maternal allele remains normally imprinted (IGF2 silenced, H19 expressed).
- **Consequence:** The net effect is *overexpression* of IGF2. IGF2 is a potent mitogen and growth factor, explaining the overgrowth features (LGA, macroglossia, omphalocele). The loss of H19 function (though H19 is non-coding, it acts as a tumor suppressor) also contributes to the increased cancer risk (Wilms tumor).
- **Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** In paternal UPD of chromosome 11p15, the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 11p15 from the father and none from the mother. Since the paternal alleles express IGF2 and silence H19, this results in biallelic expression of IGF2 and silencing of H19, leading to BWS. This matches the functional consequence of the methylation defect described (overexpression of IGF2).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal UPD of chromosome 11p15 results in inheriting two maternal copies and none paternal. This leads to biallelic expression of H19 and silencing of IGF2, which is associated with *Silver-Russell syndrome* (a growth restriction disorder), not BWS. This option incorrectly describes the UPD type and the resulting gene expression pattern.
- (C): A point mutation in the IGF2 gene causing constitutive activation would lead to IGF2 overexpression and BWS-like features. However, the vignette specifically describes an *imprinting defect* revealed by methylation analysis, not a sequence mutation. While IGF2 overexpression is the key event, the *mechanism* described in the vignette is epigenetic dysregulation, not a coding mutation.
- (D): Deletion of the maternal copy of the 11p15 region would leave only the paternal copy. The paternal allele expresses IGF2 and silences H19. This would lead to overexpression of IGF2 and silencing of H19, consistent with BWS. However, the vignette describes *loss of methylation* on the paternal allele, not a deletion of the maternal allele. While both lead to IGF2 overexpression, the specific molecular finding points to the methylation defect.
- (E): A translocation involving 11p15 and 21 would not typically lead to trisomy 11p15 (trisomy is usually for whole chromosomes, not specific regions). Even if it did somehow result in extra copies of 11p15, the imprinting status would still be crucial. Overexpression of *both* H19 and IGF2 is not the typical mechanism for BWS; BWS is primarily driven by IGF2 overexpression (and/or H19 loss of function). This option misrepresents the genetic event and the resulting gene expression pattern.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome is caused by epigenetic dysregulation of the imprinted 11p15 region, most commonly leading to IGF2 overexpression. Key features include LGA, macroglossia, omphalocele, and increased risk of Wilms tumor. The molecular basis involves loss of methylation on the paternal allele of H19 and/or IGF2. Differentiate BWS (overgrowth, IGF2 overexpression) from Silver-Russell syndrome (growth restriction, maternal UPD 11p15 or hypomethylation of the maternal allele).

<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Silver-Russell Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Silver-Russell syndrome (SRS) is an imprinting disorder caused by epigenetic or genetic alterations affecting the 11p15 region, leading to growth restriction and characteristic physical features. The primary mechanisms involve hypomethylation of the maternal allele of *H19* and *IGF2* (leading to reduced IGF2 expression) or paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7 (leading to loss of maternal *PLAG1* expression). This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis of SRS, specifically the role of imprinting and UPD.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl is brought to the pediatrician by her parents due to concerns about her growth. Since birth, she has exhibited severe intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR), resulting in a birth weight of 1.8 kg (below the 3rd percentile). Postnatally, her growth has remained significantly below the 3rd percentile for height and weight. Physical examination reveals characteristic features including relative macrocephaly (head circumference at the 10th percentile), a triangular facial profile, prominent forehead, small chin, body asymmetry (left limb shorter than the right), and clinodactyly of the fifth finger. Her developmental milestones are within normal limits. Her parents are of average height and weight, and there is no significant family history of growth disorders. Karyotyping is normal (46,XX). Methylation analysis of the *H19* gene promoter region shows decreased methylation on the maternal allele.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Deletion of the paternal allele of the *IGF2* gene.
(B) Hypomethylation of the paternal allele of the *H19* gene.
(C) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7.
(D) Hypomethylation of the maternal allele of the *H19* gene.
(E) Deletion of the maternal allele of the *PLAG1* gene.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Severe IUGR and postnatal growth restriction:** Suggests a genetic or epigenetic disorder affecting growth, potentially involving growth factors or their regulators.
*   **Characteristic physical features:** Relative macrocephaly, triangular face, prominent forehead, small chin, body asymmetry, clinodactyly are classic signs of Silver-Russell syndrome (SRS).
*   **Normal karyotype:** Rules out chromosomal aneuploidy as the primary cause.
*   **Decreased methylation of the maternal allele of the *H19* gene:** This is a key laboratory finding pointing towards an imprinting disorder affecting the 11p15 region. *H19* and *IGF2* are imprinted genes located in this region, regulated by differential methylation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Silver-Russell syndrome (SRS) is a complex genetic disorder characterized by severe growth restriction, relative macrocephaly, specific facial features, and body asymmetry. It is primarily caused by epigenetic or genetic alterations affecting the imprinted 11p15 region on chromosome 11. This region contains two paternally expressed genes, *IGF2* and *CDKN1C* (formerly p57kip2), and one maternally expressed gene, *H19*.

*   **Imprinting:** Imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 11p15 region, the maternal allele is normally methylated, silencing *IGF2* and *CDKN1C*, while allowing *H19* expression. The paternal allele is unmethylated, allowing *IGF2* and *CDKN1C* expression, while silencing *H19*.
*   **Mechanism 1: Hypomethylation of the maternal allele of *H19* and *IGF2***: This is the most common cause of SRS (~50% of cases). Loss of methylation on the maternal allele leads to silencing of the maternal *IGF2* gene (due to the insulator effect of the *H19* transcript) and activation of the maternal *H19* gene. Since *IGF2* is normally expressed only from the paternal allele, this results in a significant reduction in overall IGF2 levels, which is crucial for fetal and postnatal growth.
*   **Mechanism 2: Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7:** This accounts for about 10% of SRS cases. In maternal UPD7, the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 7 from the mother and none from the father. This leads to the loss of the paternal contribution of the *PLAG1* gene (located on 7q31), which is normally expressed only from the paternal allele. *PLAG1* is involved in growth and development.
*   **Mechanism 3: Deletion of the paternal 11p15 region:** This leads to loss of the paternal *IGF2* and *CDKN1C* alleles, resulting in reduced IGF2 expression.
*   **Mechanism 4: Maternal UPD11p15:** This is rare and leads to loss of the maternal *H19* allele and biallelic expression of *IGF2* and *CDKN1C*, which is generally incompatible with life.

In this specific case, the clinical presentation (IUGR, characteristic facies, asymmetry) is highly suggestive of SRS. The laboratory finding of *decreased methylation on the maternal allele of the H19 gene* directly points to the most common molecular mechanism underlying SRS. This hypomethylation affects the expression of both *H19* and *IGF2* on the maternal allele, leading to reduced overall IGF2 levels and subsequent growth restriction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the paternal allele of the *IGF2* gene: Deletion of the paternal *IGF2* allele would lead to reduced IGF2 expression and potentially growth restriction. However, this is a less common cause of SRS compared to maternal hypomethylation of the 11p15 region. Furthermore, the vignette specifically mentions decreased methylation of the *maternal* *H19* allele, which is the hallmark of the most common SRS mechanism. This option doesn't align with the provided lab data.
- (B) Hypomethylation of the paternal allele of the *H19* gene: The *H19* gene is normally silenced by methylation on the paternal allele. Hypomethylation of the paternal *H19* allele would lead to its expression, but this is not associated with SRS. SRS is caused by alterations affecting the *maternal* allele's methylation status or paternal chromosome loss/UPD.
- (C) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7: Maternal UPD7 leads to loss of the paternal *PLAG1* allele, causing SRS. While this is a valid cause of SRS, the vignette specifically provides evidence of altered methylation at the 11p15 locus (*H19* hypomethylation), which points towards a different mechanism. UPD7 would not cause changes in *H19* methylation patterns.
- (E) Deletion of the maternal allele of the *PLAG1* gene: The *PLAG1* gene is located on chromosome 7, not chromosome 11. Deletion of the maternal *PLAG1* allele would lead to loss of the maternal contribution, but the paternal allele would still be present and expressed. Loss of the paternal *PLAG1* allele (due to maternal UPD7) causes SRS. Deletion of the maternal *PLAG1* allele is not a known cause of SRS, and it doesn't relate to the 11p15 locus or the *H19* methylation findings.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Silver-Russell syndrome is a classic example of an imprinting disorder affecting the 11p15 region. The most common cause involves hypomethylation of the maternal allele of *H19* and *IGF2*, leading to reduced IGF2 expression and growth restriction. Key features include IUGR, relative macrocephaly, specific facial features, and body asymmetry. Remember to differentiate SRS from other causes of IUGR and short stature, such as Turner syndrome, Noonan syndrome, and constitutional growth delay. Also, distinguish the molecular mechanisms of SRS (maternal 11p15 hypomethylation, paternal UPD7, paternal 11p15 deletion) from other imprinting disorders like Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes (15q11-q13).
