<a id='question-069'></a>

### Question 069: Trithorax Group Proteins: Counteracting Polycomb Repression by Depositing Activating H3K4me3 Marks
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trithorax group (TrxG) proteins are transcriptional activators that counteract Polycomb Repressive Complex 2 (PRC2) mediated gene silencing by depositing activating histone modifications, specifically H3K4me3, at gene promoters, thereby facilitating transcription. (Trithorax 组蛋白修饰酶对抗 Polycomb 抑制复合物介导的基因沉默，通过在基因启动子处沉积激活组蛋白修饰，特别是 H3K4me3，从而促进转录。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue over the past year. Her symptoms began insidiously, initially affecting proximal limb muscles, and have gradually worsened, now impacting her ability to climb stairs and lift objects. She denies any sensory changes, pain, or skin rashes. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she has no known family history of neuromuscular disorders. Physical examination reveals moderate proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 4/5) in both upper and lower extremities, without distal weakness or atrophy. Deep tendon reflexes are normal. Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are markedly elevated at 15,000 U/L (normal < 200 U/L). An electromyogram (EMG) shows myopathic changes with short-duration, low-amplitude polyphasic motor unit potentials. Muscle biopsy reveals ragged red fibers and cytochrome c oxidase (COX) deficiency on histochemical staining. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous missense mutation in the *TWNK* gene, which encodes Twinkie, a mitochondrial helicase involved in mtDNA replication and repair. Further biochemical analysis of the patient's muscle cells shows significantly reduced levels of mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) copies compared to controls. Given the role of mtDNA copy number in mitochondrial respiratory chain function, which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for the observed reduction in mtDNA copy number in this patient's muscle cells?

(A) Increased activity of Polycomb Repressive Complex 2 (PRC2) leading to H3K27me3 deposition at mtDNA promoters.
(B) Decreased activity of Trithorax group (TrxG) proteins leading to reduced H3K4me3 deposition at mtDNA promoters.
(C) Increased activity of mitochondrial DNA methyltransferases (MT-DNMTs) leading to hypermethylation of mtDNA promoters.
(D) Decreased activity of mitochondrial DNA demethylases (MT-DDMs) leading to hypomethylation of mtDNA promoters.
(E) Increased activity of mitochondrial DNA helicases, leading to excessive unwinding and degradation of mtDNA.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with progressive proximal muscle weakness, fatigue, elevated CK, myopathic EMG, ragged red fibers, COX deficiency on muscle biopsy. This clinical picture strongly suggests a mitochondrial myopathy.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous missense mutation in *TWNK* gene. Twinkie is a mitochondrial helicase essential for mtDNA replication and maintenance. Mutations in *TWNK* are known causes of mitochondrial depletion syndromes.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Significantly reduced mtDNA copy number in muscle cells. This confirms a mitochondrial depletion syndrome, where the number of mtDNA molecules per cell is abnormally low.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the reduced mtDNA copy number, linking the *TWNK* mutation to epigenetic regulation of mtDNA.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) Replication & Maintenance:** mtDNA is a circular molecule replicated independently of the nuclear genome. Replication requires specific enzymes, including DNA polymerase gamma (Pol γ), mitochondrial helicase II (Twinkie, encoded by *TWNK*), and mitochondrial single-stranded DNA binding protein (mtSSB). Twinkie unwinds the mtDNA duplex during replication.
- **Regulation of mtDNA Copy Number:** mtDNA copy number is tightly regulated and is crucial for maintaining mitochondrial respiratory chain function. While the exact mechanisms are complex and not fully elucidated, epigenetic modifications play a role.
- **Role of Trithorax Group (TrxG) Proteins:** TrxG proteins are transcriptional activators in the nucleus, but analogous mechanisms involving histone modifications also regulate mtDNA copy number. Specifically, H3K4me3 (trimethylation of histone H3 lysine 4) is an activating mark associated with active transcription and replication origins. TrxG proteins, like MLL1/COMPASS, deposit H3K4me3.
- **Role of Polycomb Repressive Complex 2 (PRC2):** PRC2 is a transcriptional repressor complex that deposits H3K27me3 (trimethylation of histone H3 lysine 27), an inhibitory mark.
- **Mechanism in *TWNK* Deficiency:** Loss of Twinkie function impairs mtDNA replication and maintenance. This leads to a reduction in mtDNA copy number. Studies suggest that reduced mtDNA copy number can trigger a compensatory response involving epigenetic modifications. Specifically, the reduction in mtDNA replication forks or the accumulation of replication stress might lead to altered chromatin structure at mtDNA origins. This altered structure can recruit factors that promote repressive histone modifications or inhibit activating ones. Conversely, a lack of active replication forks might lead to decreased deposition of activating marks like H3K4me3. TrxG proteins are responsible for depositing H3K4me3. Therefore, reduced activity or recruitment of TrxG proteins (or factors that promote their activity) could lead to decreased H3K4me3 at mtDNA origins, contributing to the failure to maintain or replicate mtDNA, thus exacerbating the depletion. While the exact interplay is complex, the question asks for the mechanism *responsible* for the reduced mtDNA copy number. Impaired replication due to loss of Twinkie is the primary defect. However, the question implies an epigenetic regulatory mechanism is involved in maintaining mtDNA levels. Reduced H3K4me3 deposition by TrxG proteins would decrease the potential for mtDNA replication initiation and maintenance, thus contributing to the observed depletion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased activity of PRC2 leading to H3K27me3 deposition. H3K27me3 is a repressive mark. While PRC2 activity might be involved in regulating mtDNA, increased PRC2 activity leading to H3K27me3 deposition would *further* repress mtDNA replication/transcription, potentially contributing to depletion. However, the primary defect is the loss of Twinkie, which directly impairs replication. The question asks for the mechanism *responsible* for the reduction, and while PRC2 might play a role, reduced *activation* (H3K4me3) is more directly linked to the failure of replication initiation/maintenance in the context of replication stress caused by Twinkie deficiency. Furthermore, evidence suggests that mtDNA depletion can lead to *decreased* H3K27me3, not increased.
- (C): Increased activity of mitochondrial DNA methyltransferases (MT-DNMTs) leading to hypermethylation of mtDNA promoters. While mtDNA methylation occurs, it's generally considered less prevalent and less functionally characterized than histone modifications in regulating mtDNA copy number. Hypermethylation of promoters would typically lead to repression. While this could contribute, histone modifications like H3K4me3 are considered more central regulators of mtDNA replication initiation. There is limited evidence for significant MT-DNMT activity in humans, and hypermethylation is not the established mechanism for mtDNA depletion in *TWNK* deficiency.
- (D): Decreased activity of mitochondrial DNA demethylases (MT-DDMs) leading to hypomethylation of mtDNA promoters. Similar to (C), the role of mtDNA methylation is less established. Hypomethylation of promoters would generally lead to increased transcription/replication. Decreased activity of demethylases would lead to hypermethylation, not hypomethylation. This option is incorrect on multiple levels.
- (E): Increased activity of mitochondrial DNA helicases, leading to excessive unwinding and degradation of mtDNA. The patient has a *loss-of-function* mutation in *TWNK*, which encodes a mitochondrial helicase. Therefore, helicase activity is expected to be *decreased*, not increased. Increased helicase activity leading to degradation is not the mechanism associated with *TWNK* deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the role of Trithorax group proteins (TrxG) in depositing activating histone modifications (H3K4me3) to promote transcription and replication, and how their dysregulation can impact mtDNA copy number. Recognize that mitochondrial diseases can involve defects in mtDNA replication machinery (like Twinkie) and that epigenetic mechanisms involving histone modifications regulate mtDNA maintenance.
- **Differentials:** Mitochondrial depletion syndromes can be caused by mutations in various genes involved in mtDNA replication (e.g., *POLG*, *TK2*, *SUCLA2*, *TWNK*). While the primary defect is impaired replication, secondary epigenetic changes involving histone modifications can contribute to the phenotype. Differentiate this mechanism from direct mtDNA mutations or defects in oxidative phosphorylation complexes. Also, differentiate the activating H3K4me3 mark (TrxG) from the repressive H3K27me3 mark (PRC2).

<a id='question-070'></a>

### Question 070: Mixed Lineage Leukemia (MLL1 / KMT2A) Gene Rearrangements
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: MLL1 (KMT2A) gene rearrangements, particularly the t(4;11) translocation, lead to aberrant histone H3K4 methylation, resulting in altered gene expression and contributing to the pathogenesis of acute leukemias, especially in infants and young children. (MLL1基因重排，特别是t(4;11)易位，导致组蛋白H3K4甲基化异常，从而改变基因表达，并导致急性白血病的发生，尤其是在婴儿和幼儿中。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, lethargy, and easy bruising. Physical examination reveals pallor, petechiae, hepatosplenomegaly, and lymphadenopathy. Complete blood count (CBC) shows pancytopenia (WBC 2,000/µL with 80% blasts, Hb 7 g/dL, Platelets 20,000/µL). Bone marrow aspirate confirms acute leukemia with morphologic features consistent with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) with monocytic differentiation. Cytogenetic analysis of the bone marrow cells reveals a translocation between chromosomes 4 and 11, denoted as t(4;11)(q21;q23). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) using a probe specific for the MLL1 gene locus on chromosome 11q23 confirms the presence of the MLL1 gene rearrangement. Further molecular studies show increased levels of mono-methylated histone H3 lysine 4 (H3K4me1) in the leukemic blasts compared to normal bone marrow cells. The patient is started on chemotherapy, but despite initial remission, relapses occur frequently.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the altered gene expression pattern observed in this patient's leukemic blasts?

(A) Increased activity of histone deacetylases (HDACs) leading to chromatin condensation and transcriptional repression of tumor suppressor genes.
(B) Fusion of the MLL1 protein with a partner protein, creating a chimeric protein with altered histone methyltransferase activity that leads to aberrant methylation of histone H3 lysine 4.
(C) Activation of oncogenic signaling pathways, such as the RAS/MAPK pathway, leading to increased transcription of genes involved in cell proliferation and survival.
(D) Loss of function mutations in tumor suppressor genes, such as TP53 or RB1, leading to uncontrolled cell cycle progression and apoptosis evasion.
(E) Increased expression of microRNAs (miRNAs) that target mRNAs encoding key regulators of hematopoiesis, leading to dysregulation of normal blood cell development.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 2-year-old boy with symptoms (fever, lethargy, bruising), signs (pallor, petechiae, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy), and lab findings (pancytopenia with blasts) suggestive of acute leukemia.
- **Diagnosis:** Acute leukemia, morphologically AML with monocytic differentiation.
- **Cytogenetics:** t(4;11)(q21;q23) translocation. This is a characteristic translocation associated with MLL1 (KMT2A) rearrangements.
- **FISH:** Confirms MLL1 gene rearrangement at 11q23.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Increased levels of mono-methylated histone H3 lysine 4 (H3K4me1) in leukemic blasts. This is a specific consequence of MLL1 fusion proteins.
- **Clinical Course:** Frequent relapses, typical for leukemias associated with MLL1 rearrangements.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the altered gene expression.

The key clues are the t(4;11) translocation, confirmation of MLL1 rearrangement, and the finding of increased H3K4me1. These point directly to the function of the MLL1 protein and how its rearrangement affects histone modification.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **MLL1 (KMT2A):** The MLL1 gene encodes a histone methyltransferase complex subunit. This complex is crucial for regulating gene expression during development and hematopoiesis.
- **Normal Function:** The MLL1 complex normally catalyzes the mono-methylation of histone H3 lysine 4 (H3K4me1). H3K4me1 is associated with active gene transcription, particularly at enhancers and promoters.
- **t(4;11) Translocation:** This translocation fuses the MLL1 gene on chromosome 11q23 with various partner genes, most commonly the AF4 gene (also known as AFF1) on chromosome 4q21. Other partners include AF9, ELL, ENL, etc.
- **Fusion Protein:** The resulting MLL-AF4 (or other MLL-partner) fusion protein retains the histone methyltransferase activity of MLL1 but loses its normal regulation and specificity.
- **Aberrant Methylation:** The fusion protein exhibits *constitutively active* and *misdirected* H3K4 methylation. It methylates H3K4 at inappropriate genomic locations (e.g., promoters of genes that should be repressed) and often leads to an *increase* in H3K4me1 levels overall, although the specific pattern is crucial.
- **Gene Expression Changes:** This aberrant methylation pattern leads to the inappropriate activation of oncogenes (like HOXA9, MEIS1) and the repression of tumor suppressor genes, driving leukemogenesis. The increased H3K4me1 found in the patient's blasts is a direct consequence of the fusion protein's activity.
- **Clinical Relevance:** MLL1 rearrangements are found in about 5-10% of AML cases, particularly in infants and young children, and are associated with a poor prognosis and high relapse rate, as seen in this patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While histone modifications are involved, MLL1 rearrangements lead to *increased* H3K4 methylation (specifically mono-methylation), not increased HDAC activity leading to deacetylation and repression. HDAC inhibitors are sometimes used therapeutically in hematologic malignancies, but they are not the primary mechanism caused by MLL1 fusion. MLL1 fusion proteins are histone *methyl*transferases, not deacetylases.
- (C): **Incorrect.** While oncogenic signaling pathways (like RAS/MAPK) are often activated in leukemias, the primary mechanism directly resulting from the MLL1 fusion protein is altered histone methylation. The MLL1 fusion protein itself doesn't directly activate RAS/MAPK, although downstream effects might influence these pathways. The question asks for the *most direct* mechanism related to the MLL1 rearrangement and the observed H3K4me1 increase.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Loss-of-function mutations in tumor suppressors like TP53 or RB1 are common in various cancers, including some leukemias. However, the specific genetic abnormality identified in this patient is the MLL1 translocation, and the biochemical finding is altered histone methylation. While these tumor suppressors might also be affected in the leukemic cells, it's not the *direct* consequence of the MLL1 fusion protein's activity.
- (E): **Incorrect.** MicroRNAs (miRNAs) play roles in regulating gene expression in hematopoiesis and cancer. Aberrant miRNA expression can contribute to leukemia development. However, the hallmark of MLL1 rearrangements is the altered activity of the MLL1 histone methyltransferase complex and the resulting changes in histone methylation patterns, specifically H3K4me1. This is a more direct and specific mechanism linked to the identified translocation and biochemical finding.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** MLL1 (KMT2A) gene rearrangements, often involving the t(4;11) translocation, create fusion proteins with aberrant histone methyltransferase activity, leading to increased H3K4me1 and altered gene expression, driving leukemogenesis. This is a key mechanism in specific subtypes of AML, particularly in young children.
- **Differentials:** Other chromosomal translocations in AML (e.g., t(8;21) PML-RARA, t(15;17) PML-NPM1, inv(16) CBFB-MYH11) involve different genes and mechanisms. For example, t(8;21) creates a fusion protein that blocks myeloid differentiation, while inv(16) leads to abnormal core-binding factor function. MLL1 rearrangements are unique in their direct impact on histone methylation.

---

<a id='question-071'></a>

### Question 071: Epigenetic Regulation Overview: Heritable Changes in Gene Expression Without Alteration in DNA Sequence - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of epigenetic regulation, specifically DNA methylation and histone modification, and their role in gene silencing, particularly in the context of genomic imprinting and disease. (理解表观遗传调控机制，特别是DNA甲基化和组蛋白修饰，及其在基因沉默中的作用，特别是在基因组印记和疾病的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 65-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue over the past year. He has a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus and hypertension, both well-controlled with medication. His father also suffered from a similar neuromuscular disorder that began in his late 50s. Physical examination reveals proximal muscle weakness, particularly in the shoulder and pelvic girdles, and decreased deep tendon reflexes. Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are markedly elevated (10x upper limit of normal). Electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy reveals type II fiber atrophy and increased connective tissue. Genetic testing for common muscular dystrophies (e.g., Duchenne, Becker, limb-girdle) is negative. However, methylation analysis of the *MYOT* gene promoter region reveals hypermethylation in the patient's muscle tissue, which is absent in his unaffected mother and unaffected daughter. The *MYOT* gene encodes a protein crucial for muscle fiber maintenance. The patient's father had the same methylation pattern.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for the patient's condition?

(A) A point mutation in the coding sequence of the *MYOT* gene leading to a non-functional protein.
(B) A chromosomal translocation disrupting the *MYOT* gene.
(C) Genomic imprinting involving the *MYOT* gene, where the paternal allele is silenced by methylation, and the maternal allele is also silenced due to the hypermethylation observed in the patient.
(D) A deficiency in a DNA repair enzyme leading to increased mutation rates in the *MYOT* gene.
(E) A mutation in a histone deacetylase (HDAC) gene, leading to global hyperacetylation and inappropriate gene expression.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 65-year-old male with progressive proximal muscle weakness, fatigue, elevated CK, myopathic EMG, and muscle biopsy showing type II fiber atrophy. This points towards a late-onset myopathy.
*   **Family History:** Father had a similar condition starting in his late 50s, suggesting a genetic component, potentially autosomal dominant or X-linked (though less likely given the father's presentation).
*   **Negative Genetic Testing:** Standard genetic tests for common muscular dystrophies are negative, ruling out typical mutations in known disease genes.
*   **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the *MYOT* gene promoter in the patient's muscle tissue is detected. This is absent in his unaffected mother and daughter. This is the crucial clue pointing towards an epigenetic mechanism.
*   **Father's Pattern:** The patient's father also had the same methylation pattern, consistent with inheritance.
*   **Gene Function:** *MYOT* encodes a protein essential for muscle fiber maintenance. Silencing this gene would lead to muscle degeneration.
*   **Deduction:** The combination of a late-onset myopathy, negative standard genetic testing, specific hypermethylation of the *MYOT* promoter, and paternal inheritance pattern strongly suggests a disorder related to genomic imprinting or other epigenetic silencing mechanisms affecting the *MYOT* gene. The fact that the mother and daughter lack this methylation pattern further supports this.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** This is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. One allele (either maternal or paternal) is silenced, typically through DNA methylation in the promoter region or modifications of associated histones.
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** The vignette describes hypermethylation of the *MYOT* gene promoter in the patient's muscle tissue. This hypermethylation leads to transcriptional silencing of the gene. The fact that the patient's mother and daughter do not have this methylation pattern suggests that this specific methylation event is not present in the maternal lineage or is lost/modified during germline transmission or early development in the daughter. The father having the same pattern indicates paternal inheritance.
*   **Implication:** If the *MYOT* gene is subject to genomic imprinting, and the paternal allele is normally silenced, the additional hypermethylation observed in the patient could lead to silencing of *both* the paternal (normally silenced) and maternal alleles, resulting in a complete loss of *MYOT* protein function and the observed myopathy. This fits the late-onset pattern, as the silencing might become more pronounced or the compensatory mechanisms fail with age. The negative standard genetic tests are explained because the underlying defect is epigenetic (methylation pattern) rather than a change in the DNA sequence itself.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** A point mutation in the coding sequence would typically be detected by standard genetic sequencing. While it could cause a non-functional protein and myopathy, it doesn't explain the specific hypermethylation pattern observed, the negative standard genetic tests, or the inheritance pattern linked to methylation. This option represents a typical Mendelian mutation.
- (B): **Incorrect.** A chromosomal translocation disrupting the *MYOT* gene would likely be detectable by cytogenetic analysis (karyotype) or molecular techniques like FISH or PCR. It doesn't explain the specific promoter hypermethylation or the inheritance pattern linked to methylation. This option represents a structural chromosomal abnormality.
- (D): **Incorrect.** A deficiency in a DNA repair enzyme would lead to increased mutation rates throughout the genome, potentially causing various cancers or other genetic disorders. While it could theoretically lead to mutations in *MYOT*, it doesn't specifically explain the targeted hypermethylation of the *MYOT* promoter region or the inheritance pattern linked to methylation. This option represents a defect in DNA maintenance.
- (E): **Incorrect.** A mutation in a histone deacetylase (HDAC) gene would lead to decreased histone acetylation, generally resulting in increased gene silencing globally or for specific target genes. While epigenetic, this typically causes broader effects (e.g., cancer predisposition syndromes) and doesn't specifically explain the targeted hypermethylation of the *MYOT* promoter or the inheritance pattern linked to methylation. Furthermore, HDAC mutations typically lead to *hyper*acetylation, not hypermethylation. This option confuses histone modifications with DNA methylation and the specific locus affected.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation and histone modifications, are heritable changes in gene expression that occur without altering the underlying DNA sequence. Genomic imprinting is a key example where parent-of-origin-specific expression is regulated, often involving DNA methylation. Aberrant methylation patterns can lead to disease, especially when they silence essential genes. This case highlights how epigenetic changes can mimic Mendelian inheritance patterns and cause disease, often being missed by standard genetic sequencing. Key differentials include typical Mendelian disorders (like muscular dystrophies caused by sequence mutations), chromosomal abnormalities, and other epigenetic syndromes (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes, Rett syndrome).

---

<a id='question-072'></a>

### Question 072: DNA Methylation Dynamics: CpG Methylation at Promoter Regions Mediating Long-Term Gene Silencing - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of DNA methylation, specifically CpG methylation in promoter regions, as a mechanism for long-term gene silencing and its implications in genomic imprinting and disease (e.g., cancer). 了解DNA甲基化，特别是启动子区域的CpG甲基化，作为长期基因沉默的机制及其在基因组印记和疾病（例如癌症）中的作用。

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive fatigue, weight loss, and abdominal pain over the past 6 months. He has a 40-pack-year smoking history. Physical examination reveals a palpable mass in the right upper quadrant of the abdomen and jaundice. Laboratory tests show elevated serum alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) levels (1200 ng/mL, normal <10 ng/mL) and abnormal liver function tests (AST 85 U/L, ALT 92 U/L, Bilirubin 4.5 mg/dL). Imaging studies confirm a large hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) in the liver. Biopsy of the tumor tissue is performed. Molecular analysis of the tumor cells reveals a specific pattern: the *IGF2* gene is highly expressed, while the *H19* gene, located nearby on chromosome 11p15.5, is silenced. Furthermore, methylation analysis of the promoter region of the *H19* gene shows hypermethylation (increased %DNA methylation), while the promoter region of the *IGF2* gene shows hypomethylation (decreased %DNA methylation). The patient's germline DNA shows normal methylation patterns for both *IGF2* and *H19* promoters. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the differential expression of *IGF2* and *H19* in this patient's HCC?

(A) Loss of heterozygosity (LOH) at the 11p15.5 locus leading to uniparental disomy (UPD) of the paternal chromosome 11, resulting in biallelic expression of *IGF2* and silencing of *H19*.
(B) Somatic mutation in the *IGF2* gene promoter leading to loss of CpG methylation, allowing for constitutive expression of *IGF2* and subsequent downregulation of *H19* via a negative feedback loop.
(C) Aberrant DNA methylation patterns specific to HCC, where hypermethylation of the *IGF2* promoter and hypomethylation of the *H19* promoter occur, leading to silencing of *IGF2* and activation of *H19*.
(D) Activation of the *H19* gene through a mechanism independent of DNA methylation, such as histone acetylation, which then represses *IGF2* expression via RNA interference.
(E) Somatic mutation in the *H19* gene leading to a gain-of-function mutation that disrupts its normal regulatory function, resulting in silencing of *H19* and increased expression of *IGF2*.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, smoker, fatigue, weight loss, abdominal pain, jaundice, palpable RUQ mass, elevated AFP, abnormal LFTs -> Suggestive of hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC).
- **Tumor Biopsy Findings:** HCC confirmed.
- **Molecular Analysis:** *IGF2* highly expressed, *H19* silenced in tumor cells.
- **Methylation Analysis:** *H19* promoter hypermethylated, *IGF2* promoter hypomethylated in tumor cells.
- **Germline Analysis:** Normal methylation patterns for *IGF2* and *H19* promoters.
- **Key Clues:** The differential expression (*IGF2* up, *H19* down) and the specific methylation patterns (*H19* hypermethylated, *IGF2* hypomethylated) in the tumor, contrasting with normal germline patterns, point towards an epigenetic alteration in the tumor cells affecting the 11p15.5 imprinted region. The question asks for the mechanism explaining this pattern.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 11p15.5 region contains two imprinted genes, *IGF2* (Insulin-like Growth Factor 2) and *H19*. Imprinted genes exhibit parent-of-origin-specific expression. Normally, *IGF2* is expressed from the paternal allele, and *H19* is expressed from the maternal allele.
- **Mechanism of Imprinting:** This parent-of-origin-specific expression is established during gametogenesis through epigenetic modifications, primarily DNA methylation.
- **Normal State:** In the normal maternal allele, the *IGF2* promoter is heavily methylated, silencing its expression. The *H19* promoter is unmethylated, allowing its expression. In the normal paternal allele, the *IGF2* promoter is unmethylated, allowing its expression. The *H19* promoter is methylated, silencing its expression.
- **Tumorigenesis (HCC):** In some HCC cases, epigenetic alterations can disrupt this normal imprinting pattern. Loss of heterozygosity (LOH) at the 11p15.5 locus is common. If LOH occurs with retention of the paternal chromosome 11 (paternal UPD - uniparental disomy), the individual effectively has two copies of the paternal allele. Since the paternal allele normally expresses *IGF2* and silences *H19*, this leads to biallelic expression of *IGF2* (high expression) and biallelic silencing of *H19* (silenced). This scenario matches the findings in the patient's tumor.
- **Option (A) Explanation:** LOH at 11p15.5 with retention of the paternal chromosome (paternal UPD) results in two copies of the paternal allele. The paternal allele has an unmethylated *IGF2* promoter (leading to high *IGF2* expression) and a methylated *H19* promoter (leading to *H19* silencing). This perfectly explains the observed gene expression and methylation patterns in the tumor.
- **Why other options are incorrect:**
    - (B) Somatic mutation in *IGF2* promoter causing loss of methylation would lead to *IGF2* expression, but it doesn't directly explain the silencing of *H19* or the hypermethylation of the *H19* promoter. Also, the vignette states *IGF2* promoter is *hypo*methylated, not that there's a mutation causing loss of methylation.
    - (C) Hypermethylation of the *IGF2* promoter would silence *IGF2*, contradicting the finding of high *IGF2* expression.
    - (D) While histone acetylation can activate genes, the primary mechanism described in the vignette involves DNA methylation patterns specific to the *IGF2* and *H19* promoters, which are hallmarks of genomic imprinting. The vignette focuses on methylation changes.
    - (E) A gain-of-function mutation in *H19* would likely lead to *H19* activation, contradicting the finding of *H19* silencing. Furthermore, *H19* is a non-coding RNA involved in regulating *IGF2*, but its silencing is primarily mediated by methylation in this context.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the mechanism of paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) following LOH at the 11p15.5 imprinted region. This leads to two copies of the paternally inherited allele, resulting in biallelic expression of *IGF2* (paternal allele is unmethylated at the *IGF2* promoter) and biallelic silencing of *H19* (paternal allele is methylated at the *H19* promoter). This matches the observed high *IGF2* expression and low *H19* expression, along with the hypomethylation of the *IGF2* promoter and hypermethylation of the *H19* promoter in the tumor.
- (B): **Incorrect.** This option suggests a mutation in the *IGF2* promoter leading to loss of methylation. While loss of methylation at the *IGF2* promoter (hypomethylation) is observed, it's typically an epigenetic change, not a mutation *causing* the loss of methylation. More importantly, this mechanism doesn't explain the hypermethylation and silencing of *H19*. The vignette describes *hypo*methylation of the *IGF2* promoter, which is consistent with its expression from the paternal allele, not a mutation causing loss of methylation.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This option proposes hypermethylation of the *IGF2* promoter, which would lead to silencing of *IGF2*, directly contradicting the finding of high *IGF2* expression in the tumor. The observed pattern is *hypo*methylation of the *IGF2* promoter.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While histone modifications play a role in gene regulation, the primary mechanism described in the vignette and central to genomic imprinting at the 11p15.5 locus is differential DNA methylation. The vignette specifically highlights the methylation status of the *IGF2* and *H19* promoters. Furthermore, the silencing of *H19* is directly linked to hypermethylation of its promoter, not activation of *H19* via histone acetylation.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This option suggests a gain-of-function mutation in *H19*. However, the findings show *H19* is silenced, not activated. Silencing of *H19* is associated with hypermethylation of its promoter, as observed in the tumor. A gain-of-function mutation would lead to increased *H19* activity, which is the opposite of what is found.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting is regulated by differential DNA methylation patterns established during gametogenesis. Alterations in these patterns, such as loss of methylation at an imprinted locus or uniparental disomy, can lead to aberrant gene expression and contribute to tumorigenesis. Key imprinted loci include 11p15.5 (*IGF2/H19*) and 15q11-q13 (*SNRPN/UBE3A*). Understanding the molecular basis of imprinting and its disruption in diseases like HCC is crucial. Differential diagnosis includes other causes of HCC (viral hepatitis, alcohol abuse) and other genetic syndromes associated with cancer predisposition (e.g., Li-Fraumeni syndrome).

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Rett Syndrome: MeCP2 Gene Mutation, Methylated DNA Binding, and HDAC Recruitment
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rett syndrome is an X-linked dominant neurodevelopmental disorder caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene, which encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2). MeCP2 binds to methylated CpG islands in DNA, primarily in neurons, and recruits transcriptional corepressor complexes, including histone deacetylases (HDACs), leading to chromatin condensation and gene silencing. Loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2* disrupt this process, leading to abnormal brain development and function. (Rett 综合征是一种 X 连锁显性神经发育障碍，由 *MECP2* 基因突变引起，该基因编码甲基胞嘧啶结合蛋白 2 (MeCP2)。MeCP2 结合 DNA 中的甲基化 CpG 岛，主要在神经元中，并募集转录共抑制复合物，包括组蛋白去乙酰化酶 (HDAC)，导致染色质凝缩和基因沉默。*MECP2* 基因失活突变会破坏这一过程，导致大脑发育和功能异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 16-month-old female presents to her pediatrician with a history of normal development for the first 6-12 months, followed by a period of regression. Her parents report loss of purposeful hand skills (e.g., no longer waves bye-bye or reaches for objects), slowed head growth (microcephaly), and the development of stereotypic hand movements (e.g., hand-wringing, clapping). She also exhibits gait ataxia and reduced expressive language. Family history is unremarkable except for a maternal uncle with intellectual disability. Physical examination reveals microcephaly (head circumference at the 3rd percentile), hypotonia, and characteristic stereotypic hand movements. Neurological examination shows significant developmental delay and ataxia. Chromosomal microarray analysis is normal. Targeted sequencing of the *MECP2* gene reveals a novel missense mutation (c.1234G>A; p.Gly412Asp) in exon 10. Biochemical analysis of nuclear protein extracts from cultured fibroblasts shows significantly reduced binding of MeCP2 protein to methylated DNA compared to controls. Further analysis demonstrates decreased recruitment of HDAC1 and HDAC2 to target gene promoters in the patient's fibroblasts compared to controls.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the pathophysiology underlying this patient's condition?

(A) The *MECP2* mutation leads to increased acetylation of histones at target gene promoters, resulting in transcriptional activation and overexpression of genes critical for neuronal development.
(B) The *MECP2* mutation impairs the ability of the MeCP2 protein to bind to methylated DNA, preventing the recruitment of transcriptional activators and leading to widespread gene silencing.
(C) The *MECP2* mutation results in a gain-of-function effect, where the mutant MeCP2 protein binds to unmethylated DNA, leading to aberrant chromatin remodeling and activation of oncogenes.
(D) The *MECP2* mutation causes the MeCP2 protein to misfold and aggregate in the endoplasmic reticulum, leading to endoplasmic reticulum stress and activation of the unfolded protein response, ultimately causing neuronal dysfunction.
(E) The *MECP2* mutation leads to decreased expression of microRNAs (miRNAs) that normally target mRNAs encoding synaptic proteins, resulting in impaired synaptic plasticity and neuronal communication.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 16-month-old female with initial normal development followed by regression (loss of hand skills, slowed head growth, stereotypic movements, ataxia, language delay). This clinical picture is highly suggestive of Rett syndrome.
*   **Family History:** Maternal uncle with intellectual disability is consistent with X-linked inheritance, although Rett syndrome is typically sporadic due to *de novo* mutations.
*   **Physical Exam:** Microcephaly, hypotonia, stereotypic hand movements are classic features of Rett syndrome.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Targeted sequencing reveals a novel missense mutation (c.1234G>A; p.Gly412Asp) in the *MECP2* gene, confirming the diagnosis of Rett syndrome.
*   **Biochemical Analysis:** Reduced binding of MeCP2 protein to methylated DNA in fibroblasts indicates a loss-of-function effect of the mutation on MeCP2's primary function.
*   **Molecular Analysis:** Decreased recruitment of HDAC1 and HDAC2 to target gene promoters in the patient's fibroblasts further supports the loss of MeCP2's role in recruiting corepressor complexes.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathophysiology.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Rett Syndrome and MeCP2:** Rett syndrome is primarily caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene located on the X chromosome (Xq28). *MECP2* encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2).
*   **MeCP2 Function:** MeCP2 is a transcriptional regulator. It specifically binds to methylated cytosines within CpG dinucleotides in DNA. These methylated CpG sites are often found in the promoter regions of genes.
*   **MeCP2 Recruitment of Corepressors:** Once bound to methylated DNA, MeCP2 recruits various proteins, including histone deacetylases (HDACs) like HDAC1 and HDAC2, as well as other corepressor complexes (e.g., NuRD complex, Sin3A).
*   **Chromatin Condensation and Gene Silencing:** HDACs remove acetyl groups from histone tails (primarily H3 and H4). Histone acetylation is generally associated with open chromatin and active transcription. Deacetylation leads to chromatin condensation (heterochromatin formation) and transcriptional repression (gene silencing).
*   **Pathophysiology in Rett Syndrome:** Loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2* (like the p.Gly412Asp mutation described) impair its ability to bind methylated DNA and/or recruit corepressor complexes. This leads to:
    *   Reduced recruitment of HDACs to target gene promoters.
    *   Decreased histone deacetylation.
    *   Less chromatin condensation.
    *   Increased histone acetylation at target gene promoters.
    *   Transcriptional activation (upregulation) of genes that are normally repressed by MeCP2.
    *   Dysregulation of gene expression, particularly affecting neuronal development, maturation, and function.
*   **The vignette provides direct evidence:** The patient has a *MECP2* mutation, reduced MeCP2 binding to methylated DNA, and decreased recruitment of HDACs. These findings point to a loss of MeCP2's repressive function. The clinical symptoms (regression, stereotypic movements, microcephaly) are characteristic of Rett syndrome resulting from this dysregulated gene expression.
*   **Therefore, the mechanism is:** The *MECP2* mutation leads to reduced recruitment of HDACs, resulting in decreased histone deacetylation and increased histone acetylation at target gene promoters. This leads to transcriptional activation and overexpression of genes normally silenced by MeCP2, contributing to the pathophysiology of Rett syndrome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This option describes the *opposite* of what happens. The mutation leads to *decreased* recruitment of HDACs, resulting in *decreased* histone deacetylation and *increased* histone acetylation, leading to *activation* of genes normally repressed by MeCP2. The vignette explicitly states decreased HDAC recruitment.
- (B): **Incorrect.** This option describes a scenario where MeCP2 cannot bind methylated DNA, preventing the recruitment of *transcriptional activators*. MeCP2 recruits *transcriptional corepressors* (like HDACs), not activators. Furthermore, the vignette shows reduced MeCP2 binding to methylated DNA, which is consistent with the mutation, but the consequence is decreased recruitment of *corepressors* (HDACs), not activators.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This option suggests a gain-of-function mutation where mutant MeCP2 binds *unmethylated* DNA and activates oncogenes. Rett syndrome is caused by loss-of-function mutations, not gain-of-function. MeCP2 binds *methylated* DNA, not unmethylated DNA. While aberrant gene expression occurs, the primary mechanism involves loss of repression, not activation of oncogenes via binding unmethylated DNA.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While protein misfolding and ER stress can be consequences of some mutations, the primary known mechanism of Rett syndrome pathophysiology involves the loss of MeCP2's function as a transcriptional regulator binding methylated DNA and recruiting corepressors. The vignette provides direct biochemical evidence supporting this mechanism (reduced DNA binding, reduced HDAC recruitment), not ER stress.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While miRNAs are involved in neuronal function and Rett syndrome pathophysiology is complex, the primary, direct molecular mechanism described in the vignette and known for MeCP2 involves its role in binding methylated DNA and recruiting HDACs to regulate gene expression at the transcriptional level. The vignette focuses on this specific pathway.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rett syndrome is caused by loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2*, which normally binds methylated DNA and recruits HDACs to repress target genes. Loss of this function leads to increased histone acetylation and activation of genes normally silenced by MeCP2, causing neurodevelopmental regression. Key features include regression after normal development, stereotypic hand movements, microcephaly, and X-linked inheritance (though often *de novo*). Differential diagnoses include other neurodevelopmental disorders like Angelman syndrome (UBE3A mutation, maternal deletion/imprinting defect), Fragile X syndrome (FMR1 mutation, CGG repeat expansion), and other genetic syndromes causing intellectual disability and developmental delay. Understanding the specific molecular role of MeCP2 in transcriptional repression is crucial.

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Rett Syndrome: X-Linked Dominant Mutations in MeCP2 Causing Developmental Regression, Hand-Wringing, and Apnea - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rett syndrome is an X-linked dominant neurodevelopmental disorder primarily affecting females, caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene, leading to developmental regression, characteristic stereotypic hand movements, gait abnormalities, seizures, and respiratory dysfunction. The mechanism involves loss of MeCP2 protein function, which acts as a transcriptional regulator. (Rett 综合征是一种 X 连锁显性神经发育障碍，主要影响女性，由 *MECP2* 基因突变引起，导致发育倒退、特征性刻板手部动作、步态异常、癫痫发作和呼吸功能障碍。其机制涉及 MeCP2 蛋白功能的丧失，该蛋白充当转录调节因子。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-month-old female presents to the pediatrician with concerns about developmental regression. She was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy and achieved early milestones normally (sitting at 6 months, walking at 12 months). However, over the past 6 months, her parents have noted a loss of purposeful hand skills (no longer waves bye-bye or points), development of stereotypic hand-wringing movements, difficulty walking (wide-based gait, frequent falls), and increased episodes of hyperventilation followed by breath-holding (apnea), particularly during sleep. Her head growth decelerated after the first year, resulting in acquired microcephaly. Neurological examination reveals spasticity in the lower extremities and absent speech. Her mother has no significant medical history, but her maternal grandfather had intellectual disability and died young. Karyotype analysis shows a normal female pattern (46,XX). Targeted sequencing of the *MECP2* gene reveals a novel missense mutation (c.1234G>A; p.Gly412Asp) in exon 8, predicted to be pathogenic.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Loss-of-function mutation in the *FMR1* gene leading to impaired translation of mRNA due to expanded CGG repeats, resulting in fragile X syndrome.
(B) Gain-of-function mutation in the *MECP2* gene leading to increased transcriptional repression of target genes involved in neuronal development and function.
(C) Loss-of-function mutation in the *MECP2* gene leading to decreased transcriptional activation of target genes involved in neuronal maturation and synaptic plasticity.
(D) Loss-of-function mutation in the *SHANK3* gene leading to impaired postsynaptic density protein function, resulting in Phelan-McDermid syndrome.
(E) Gain-of-function mutation in the *HTT* gene leading to expanded CAG repeats and formation of mutant huntingtin protein aggregates, resulting in Huntington disease.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-month-old female. Rett syndrome predominantly affects females.
- **Developmental History:** Normal early milestones followed by regression (loss of purposeful hand skills, loss of speech). This is a hallmark of Rett syndrome.
- **Characteristic Features:** Stereotypic hand movements (hand-wringing), gait abnormalities (wide-based gait, falls), respiratory issues (hyperventilation, apnea), acquired microcephaly. These are classic features of Rett syndrome.
- **Family History:** Maternal grandfather with intellectual disability. Rett syndrome is X-linked dominant; affected males usually have severe symptoms and often die early, while carrier females may have milder symptoms or be asymptomatic. The grandfather's history could suggest an X-linked inheritance pattern.
- **Genetic Findings:** Normal karyotype (46,XX) rules out chromosomal abnormalities like aneuploidy. Targeted sequencing reveals a pathogenic missense mutation (c.1234G>A; p.Gly412Asp) in the *MECP2* gene. This confirms the diagnosis of Rett syndrome.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism underlying the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Rett syndrome is caused by mutations in the *MECP2* gene located on the X chromosome (Xq28).
- *MECP2* encodes methyl-CpG-binding protein 2 (MeCP2).
- MeCP2 is a transcriptional regulator. It binds to methylated CpG islands in DNA.
- MeCP2 primarily functions as a transcriptional *repressor* by recruiting corepressor complexes (e.g., histone deacetylases, NuRD complex) to target genes.
- However, MeCP2 also plays a role in transcriptional *activation* by interacting with transcriptional activators (e.g., histone acetyltransferases, hCRM1/ATRX complex) at specific gene loci.
- Loss-of-function mutations in *MECP2* (as seen in this patient with a pathogenic missense mutation) lead to a deficiency or absence of functional MeCP2 protein.
- The absence of MeCP2 disrupts the normal balance of transcriptional regulation. While MeCP2 is often thought of as a repressor, its loss leads to *decreased* activation of certain target genes crucial for neuronal maturation, synaptic plasticity, and brain development. This dysregulation of gene expression, particularly the loss of activation of specific genes, is thought to be a key pathogenic mechanism.
- The clinical features (developmental regression, stereotypic movements, respiratory problems, microcephaly) arise from the widespread effects of this transcriptional dysregulation in the developing brain.
- Therefore, the primary molecular mechanism is a loss-of-function of the MeCP2 protein, leading to decreased transcriptional activation of critical neuronal genes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): *FMR1* gene mutations cause Fragile X syndrome. This is an X-linked disorder characterized by intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorder, and specific physical features. The mutation involves expanded CGG repeats in the 5' UTR, leading to methylation and silencing of the *FMR1* gene, resulting in loss of FMRP protein, which is involved in mRNA transport and translation. This mechanism and clinical presentation are distinct from Rett syndrome.
- (B): Rett syndrome is caused by *loss-of-function* mutations in *MECP2*, not gain-of-function. While MeCP2 is primarily a repressor, its loss leads to dysregulation including decreased activation of certain genes. A gain-of-function mutation would imply increased activity, which is not the case in typical Rett syndrome. Some rare atypical Rett phenotypes might involve gain-of-function, but the classic presentation and the most common mechanism involve loss-of-function.
- (D): *SHANK3* gene mutations cause Phelan-McDermid syndrome (22q13 deletion syndrome). This syndrome is characterized by intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorder, hypotonia, and distinctive facial features. SHANK3 is a postsynaptic density protein crucial for excitatory synapse formation and function. The mechanism involves haploinsufficiency or dominant-negative effects of mutated SHANK3. This is unrelated to Rett syndrome.
- (E): *HTT* gene mutations cause Huntington disease. This is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder characterized by chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms. The mutation involves expanded CAG repeats in the *HTT* gene, leading to a mutant huntingtin protein with an expanded polyglutamine tract. This protein forms aggregates and disrupts neuronal function. This mechanism and clinical presentation are distinct from Rett syndrome.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rett syndrome is a classic example of an X-linked dominant disorder caused by a loss-of-function mutation in *MECP2*, leading to severe neurodevelopmental regression in females. The key molecular mechanism involves the loss of MeCP2's role as a transcriptional regulator, disrupting neuronal maturation and function. Differentiate Rett syndrome from other X-linked intellectual disability syndromes like Fragile X syndrome (FMR1 mutation, CGG repeat expansion, translational defect) and other neurodevelopmental disorders like Phelan-McDermid syndrome (SHANK3 mutation) and Huntington disease (HTT mutation, CAG repeat expansion, protein aggregation).

<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Genomic Imprinting - Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting leads to monoallelic gene expression based on the parent of origin. Deletions or mutations in imprinted regions can cause specific syndromes, like Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) due to loss of paternally expressed genes on chromosome 15q11-q13. (基因组印记导致基于亲本来源的单等位基因表达。印记区域的缺失或突变会导致特定的综合征，例如Prader-Willi综合征 (PWS)，这是由于15q11-q13染色体上父本表达基因的缺失造成的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating (hyperphagia). The child was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy. His birth weight was normal, but he exhibited poor suckling and feeding difficulties in infancy, requiring specialized formula. He has mild intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (narrow forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip), and short stature. His parents are of average height and have no history of similar symptoms. Karyotyping reveals a normal male chromosome complement (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that the paternal allele is absent, while the maternal allele is present. Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region confirms hypermethylation on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele (which is absent).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 21.
(B) Deletion of the paternal-specific imprinted region on chromosome 15q11-q13.
(C) Mutation in the maternal allele of the *FMR1* gene, causing fragile X syndrome.
(D) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, leading to Angelman syndrome.
(E) Deletion of the maternal-specific imprinted region on chromosome 15q11-q13.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia, feeding difficulties in infancy, mild intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, short stature. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
*   **Family History:** Parents are unaffected, suggesting a *de novo* event or a condition not typically inherited in a simple Mendelian pattern.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidies like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or Klinefelter syndrome.
*   **FISH Analysis:** Absence of the paternal allele for the SNRPN gene (located in the 15q11-q13 region) confirms a loss of genetic material from the paternal chromosome 15. SNRPN is one of the key genes implicated in PWS.
*   **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele (which is absent) is the characteristic epigenetic pattern for the 15q11-q13 region. Normally, the paternal allele is hypomethylated and expressed, while the maternal allele is hypermethylated and silenced. The absence of the paternal allele coupled with the normal methylation pattern on the remaining maternal allele confirms the loss of the paternal contribution in this region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a classic example of a disorder caused by genomic imprinting. The 15q11-q13 region contains several genes, including *SNRPN*, *NKX1*, *MAGP1*, and *IPW*. These genes are normally expressed only from the paternal allele due to epigenetic modifications (DNA methylation) that silence the maternal allele. This phenomenon is called genomic imprinting.

In PWS, the critical event is the loss of function of the paternally expressed genes in this region. This can occur through several mechanisms:
1.  **Deletion:** The most common cause (~70%) is a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region.
2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** In ~25% of cases, the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal alleles in the 15q11-q13 region are normally silenced by methylation, the individual lacks expression of the paternally required genes.
3.  **Imprinting Defect:** In a small percentage of cases (~5%), there is a defect in the imprinting process itself, leading to silencing of the paternal allele, even though it is present.

The clinical vignette describes a patient with classic PWS symptoms. The FISH analysis shows the absence of the paternal allele at the SNRPN locus (within 15q11-q13). The methylation analysis confirms the normal imprinted pattern (maternal hypermethylated, paternal hypomethylated, but the paternal allele is missing). Therefore, the most likely mechanism is the deletion of the paternal-specific imprinted region on chromosome 15q11-q13.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21 leads to two copies of chromosome 21 from the mother and none from the father. This results in Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), characterized by distinct facial features, intellectual disability, congenital heart defects, etc., which are different from the patient's presentation. The FISH result also points to chromosome 15, not 21.
- (C): Mutation in the maternal allele of the *FMR1* gene causes fragile X syndrome. Fragile X syndrome is an X-linked dominant disorder characterized by intellectual disability, long face, large ears, macroorchidism (in males), and behavioral issues. It is caused by a CGG repeat expansion in the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome. The patient is male, has symptoms consistent with PWS, and the genetic defect is on chromosome 15, not the X chromosome.
- (D): Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 leads to Angelman syndrome (AS). In AS, the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. Since the paternal alleles in the 15q11-q13 region are normally expressed, and the maternal alleles are silenced, the individual lacks expression of the maternally required gene *UBE3A*. AS is characterized by severe intellectual disability, seizures, ataxia, happy demeanor, and characteristic facial features, which differ from the patient's presentation. The vignette describes loss of the *paternal* allele, leading to PWS, not AS.
- (E): Deletion of the maternal-specific imprinted region on chromosome 15q11-q13 causes Angelman syndrome (AS). This deletion removes the maternal allele of *UBE3A* and other maternally expressed genes in the region. As explained in (D), AS has a distinct clinical presentation from PWS. The patient's symptoms and genetic findings (loss of paternal allele) are consistent with PWS, not AS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting results in monoallelic expression based on parent of origin. Loss of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), while loss of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region causes Angelman syndrome (AS). PWS features include hypotonia, hyperphagia, developmental delay, and characteristic facies. AS features include severe intellectual disability, seizures, ataxia, and a happy demeanor. Key differentials include other causes of developmental delay and hypotonia (e.g., Down syndrome, spinal muscular atrophy) and other imprinting disorders.

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Prader-Willi Syndrome: Maternal 15q11-q13 Imprinted/Silenced with Paternal Deletion or Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and its role in Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), specifically the differential diagnosis between paternal deletion and maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) based on clinical features and molecular testing results. (基因组印记及其在Prader-Willi综合征中的作用，特别是基于临床特征和分子检测结果区分父源缺失和母源单亲二倍体(UPD)的鉴别诊断。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating. He was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy. His birth weight was low (2.5 kg), and he exhibited significant feeding difficulties in infancy, requiring nasogastric tube feeding for the first 6 months. Since then, he has developed hyperphagia and obesity. He has mild intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (narrow forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip), and short stature. His parents report he often falls asleep during the day but has difficulty sleeping at night. His older sister is unaffected. Karyotyping reveals a normal male karyotype (46,XY). Methylation analysis of the SNRPN gene region on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows a completely methylated pattern, consistent with maternal inheritance. Further investigation using microsatellite markers (SNRPN locus) confirms that all tested markers are inherited from his mother.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region.
(B) Maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region.
(C) Imprinting defect affecting the paternal allele.
(D) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(E) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Clinical Presentation:** The patient exhibits classic features of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS): hypotonia and feeding difficulties in infancy, followed by hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay/intellectual disability, short stature, characteristic facial features, and sleep disturbances.
- **Family History:** Unaffected older sister suggests a de novo event or a mechanism not directly inherited from parents (like UPD or imprinting defect).
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out large chromosomal abnormalities like translocations involving chromosome 15 that might disrupt the region.
- **Methylation Analysis (SNRPN):** Completely methylated pattern indicates maternal inheritance/silencing. In normal individuals, the paternal allele at the 15q11-q13 region is unmethylated and expressed, while the maternal allele is methylated and silenced due to genomic imprinting. A completely methylated pattern means the *paternal* allele is also methylated and silenced, or the patient only has maternal chromosomes in this region.
- **Microsatellite Markers (SNRPN locus):** Confirmation that *all* markers are inherited from the mother definitively points to maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15. This means the patient inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from his mother and none from his father.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes located in the 15q11-q13 region. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, a phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. Normally, the paternal copy of genes in this region (like SNRPN, MKBPBP, MAGNE1) is expressed, while the maternal copy is silenced by methylation.
- **Paternal Deletion:** This is the most common cause (~70%) of PWS. The paternal copy of the critical region is deleted, leaving only the methylated, silenced maternal copy. Methylation analysis would show a completely methylated pattern, but microsatellite analysis would show loss of paternal markers.
- **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** This occurs in ~25% of PWS cases. The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since both maternal copies are methylated and silenced, there is no functional paternal allele. Methylation analysis shows a completely methylated pattern, and microsatellite analysis shows that all markers are maternally inherited.
- **Imprinting Defect:** This occurs in ~5% of PWS cases. The methylation pattern on the paternal chromosome 15 is abnormal (hypomethylated or unmethylated), leading to silencing of the paternal allele, similar to the maternal allele. Methylation analysis shows a completely methylated pattern, and microsatellite analysis shows both paternal and maternal markers present (normal biparental inheritance).
- **Maternal Deletion:** This causes Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. In AS, the maternal copy of UBE3A is deleted or non-functional, leaving only the paternal copy, which is normally silenced in the brain.

In this case, the patient has a completely methylated pattern at the SNRPN locus (indicating loss of paternal expression) *and* all markers are inherited from the mother. This combination is diagnostic for maternal UPD for chromosome 15.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region: This is a common cause of PWS. It would result in a completely methylated pattern on methylation analysis, but microsatellite analysis would show *loss of paternal markers*, not inheritance of all markers from the mother. This contradicts the findings.
- (B) Maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region: This causes Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and happy demeanor. The clinical presentation described is classic PWS. Furthermore, a maternal deletion would leave the paternal allele unmethylated and expressed (if no imprinting defect), which is inconsistent with the completely methylated pattern observed.
- (C) Imprinting defect affecting the paternal allele: This also causes PWS. It would result in a completely methylated pattern (paternal allele silenced) on methylation analysis. However, microsatellite analysis would show *both* paternal and maternal markers present, indicating normal biparental inheritance, which contradicts the finding that all markers are maternally inherited.
- (D) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15: This is extremely rare and causes Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. This would lead to loss of the maternal UBE3A allele (if maternal deletion or imprinting defect is present) or its silencing, causing AS. The clinical picture and molecular findings (maternal inheritance of markers) are inconsistent with paternal UPD.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi syndrome results from the lack of paternal contribution from the 15q11-q13 imprinted region. The key diagnostic modalities are methylation analysis and microsatellite marker analysis. A completely methylated pattern indicates loss of paternal expression. Distinguishing between paternal deletion, maternal UPD, and imprinting defect requires microsatellite analysis: loss of paternal markers points to deletion, all markers from mother points to maternal UPD, and presence of both sets of markers points to imprinting defect. Angelman syndrome results from loss of maternal contribution from the same region and presents differently. (Prader-Willi综合征是由于15q11-q13印记区域缺乏父源贡献造成的。关键诊断方法是甲基化分析和微卫星标记分析。完全甲基化模式表明父源表达缺失。区分父源缺失、母源单亲二倍体和印记缺陷需要微卫星分析：父源标记缺失提示缺失，所有标记来自母亲提示母源UPD，两组标记均存在提示印记缺陷。Angelman综合征是由于同一区域缺乏母源贡献造成的，临床表现不同。)

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Angelman Syndrome: Paternal 15q11-q13 Imprinted/Silenced with Maternal UBE3A Deletion or Paternal UPD - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy (UPD) leading to loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene, causing Angelman syndrome. (基因组印记和单亲二体畸 (UPD) 导致 *UBE3A* 基因功能丧失，引起 Angelman 综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, severe intellectual disability, ataxia, seizures (primarily tonic-clonic), and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter (happy puppet syndrome). She has minimal speech and exhibits hand-flapping movements. Her parents report that she was born full-term without complications. Her father is 45 years old, and her mother is 32 years old. The father has a history of hypertension, managed with lisinopril. The mother has no significant medical history. Family history is unremarkable for similar neurological conditions. Physical examination reveals microcephaly, a wide mouth, prominent chin, and hypopigmentation compared to her parents. A standard G-banded karyotype shows a normal 46,XX female karyotype. Given the clinical presentation, further genetic testing is ordered. Methylation analysis of the *SNRPN* locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 reveals a pattern consistent with maternal inheritance (i.e., methylation pattern is similar to that seen in unaffected individuals). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the patient has two copies of the paternal chromosome 15. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region, including the *UBE3A* gene.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(C) Imprinting defect leading to biallelic expression of the paternal *UBE3A* allele.
(D) Maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region, including the *UBE3A* gene.
(E) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Clinical Presentation:** The patient exhibits classic features of Angelman syndrome: developmental delay, severe intellectual disability, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet syndrome"), minimal speech, hand-flapping, microcephaly, wide mouth, prominent chin, and hypopigmentation. These symptoms strongly suggest Angelman syndrome.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX karyotype rules out aneuploidy or large chromosomal rearrangements visible by standard G-banding.
- **Methylation Analysis:** The *SNRPN* locus on 15q11-q13 is used as a marker for imprinting in this region. A methylation pattern consistent with maternal inheritance indicates that the paternal allele is silenced (methylated) and the maternal allele is active (unmethylated). This is the normal pattern in unaffected individuals.
- **FISH Analysis:** FISH analysis shows two copies of the paternal chromosome 15. This indicates that the patient has inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from her father (paternal UPD) or has a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15.
- **Deduction:** Angelman syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that only one allele (the maternal allele for *UBE3A*) is normally expressed in certain brain regions. The methylation analysis shows a normal maternal pattern (paternal allele silenced). The FISH analysis shows two paternal copies of chromosome 15. If the patient had paternal UPD (option E), the methylation pattern would show a paternal pattern (maternal allele silenced, paternal allele active), which contradicts the findings. Therefore, the patient must have inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from her father, but one of them must be missing the maternal contribution (i.e., a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15). This results in the absence of the normally active maternal *UBE3A* allele and the presence of a silenced paternal *UBE3A* allele, leading to Angelman syndrome. This scenario corresponds to maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Angelman Syndrome Pathophysiology:** Angelman syndrome (AS) is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region is subject to genomic imprinting. This means that the expression of certain genes in this region depends on their parental origin. The *UBE3A* gene is imprinted, with the maternal allele being predominantly expressed in the brain, while the paternal allele is largely silenced through methylation.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of AS:** Several genetic mechanisms can lead to AS:
    1.  **Maternal Deletion (approx. 70%):** Deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome 15. This removes the active maternal *UBE3A* allele.
    2.  **Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (approx. 3-7%):** Inheritance of both copies of chromosome 15 from the father. In this case, both *UBE3A* alleles are paternally derived and silenced by methylation, leading to a lack of functional *UBE3A* protein.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect (approx. 3-7%):** A defect in the imprinting mechanism that leads to silencing of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, similar to the paternal allele. This results in the loss of maternal *UBE3A* expression.
    4.  **UBE3A Mutation (approx. 10-15%):** A mutation within the *UBE3A* gene itself on the maternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein.
    5.  **Unknown (approx. 10-15%):** The cause is not identified.
- **Interpretation of Findings:**
    - **Clinical Features:** Consistent with AS.
    - **Normal Karyotype:** Rules out large deletions or translocations detectable by G-banding.
    - **Methylation Analysis (Maternal Pattern):** Indicates that the paternal allele is silenced (methylated), which is the normal state. This rules out a paternal deletion or a paternal UPD, as these would lead to a predominantly paternal methylation pattern.
    - **FISH Analysis (Two Paternal Chromosomes):** This finding, combined with the maternal methylation pattern, strongly suggests that the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from her father, but the maternal chromosome 15 is missing. This is consistent with maternal UPD for the 15q11-q13 region or a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15. However, the question asks for the mechanism responsible for the *condition* (AS), which is the loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele. Given the FISH result (two paternal copies), the most likely scenario is a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15 encompassing the 15q11-q13 region. If it were paternal UPD, the methylation pattern would be paternal. Therefore, the maternal deletion is the most fitting explanation for the combination of clinical features, normal methylation pattern (indicating paternal silencing), and FISH showing two paternal chromosomes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region, including the *UBE3A* gene.** This would cause Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), not Angelman syndrome. PWS results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the same region (e.g., *SNRPN*, *NDN*). Furthermore, a paternal deletion would lead to a predominantly paternal methylation pattern, contradicting the findings.
- (B): **Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.** If the patient inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from her mother, the methylation pattern would be predominantly maternal (maternal allele active, paternal allele silenced), which is normal. This would not cause Angelman syndrome because the active maternal *UBE3A* allele would be present.
- (C): **Imprinting defect leading to biallelic expression of the paternal *UBE3A* allele.** An imprinting defect causing silencing of the maternal allele would lead to AS. However, this would typically result in a methylation pattern that is abnormal (e.g., methylation on the maternal allele), not the normal maternal pattern described. Also, FISH would show one maternal and one paternal chromosome 15.
- (E): **Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.** This is a known cause of Angelman syndrome. However, if the patient had paternal UPD, the methylation pattern would show a predominantly paternal pattern (maternal allele silenced, paternal allele active), which contradicts the finding of a maternal methylation pattern.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene due to genomic imprinting. The most common cause is maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region. Other causes include paternal UPD, imprinting defects, and *UBE3A* mutations. Prader-Willi syndrome is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the same region and is typically caused by paternal deletion or maternal UPD. Understanding genomic imprinting and its associated disorders is crucial. Key differential: Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) vs. Angelman Syndrome (AS). PWS involves loss of paternal contribution (deletion or maternal UPD) leading to loss of *SNRPN*, *NDN* function; AS involves loss of maternal contribution (deletion or paternal UPD) leading to loss of *UBE3A* function. Methylation analysis is key to differentiating these and other imprinting disorders.
