<a id='question-095'></a>

### Question 095: Genomic Imprinting - Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting leads to monoallelic gene expression, where only the allele inherited from one parent (maternal or paternal) is expressed, while the other allele is silenced. Deletions or mutations in imprinted regions can cause specific syndromes depending on the parent of origin. (基因组印记导致单亲基因表达，其中仅来自一个亲本（母亲或父亲）的等位基因被表达，而另一个等位基因被沉默。印记区域的缺失或突变会导致特定的综合征，具体取决于亲本来源。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating (hyperphagia). The child was born full-term but exhibited poor suckling and feeding difficulties in infancy, requiring specialized nutritional support. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, hypogonadism (small testes), and characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. His developmental milestones are significantly delayed, particularly in motor skills and speech. His parents are of average height and have no history of similar symptoms. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that the paternal allele is absent in the patient's cells.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's condition?

(A) A mutation in the maternal allele of the *UBE3A* gene, leading to Angelman syndrome.
(B) A deletion of the maternal allele of the *SNRPN* gene, leading to Prader-Willi syndrome.
(C) A deletion of the paternal allele of the *SNRPN* gene, leading to Prader-Willi syndrome.
(D) A translocation involving chromosome 21, leading to Down syndrome.
(E) A mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, leading to achondroplasia.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia, short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism, characteristic facial features (almond eyes, narrow forehead), poor suckling/feeding difficulties in infancy. This constellation of symptoms strongly suggests Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Family History:** Parents are unaffected, suggesting a de novo event or a condition not typically inherited in a simple Mendelian pattern.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidies like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or Klinefelter syndrome.
- **FISH Analysis:** Absence of the paternal allele for the *SNRPN* gene locus (15q11-q13) is the critical finding. The *SNRPN* gene is located within the imprinted region on chromosome 15.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the condition.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a classic example of a disorder caused by genomic imprinting.
- The critical region for PWS is located on the long arm of chromosome 15 (15q11-q13).
- This region contains several genes, including *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), *NKX1*, *MAGP1*, *MKX116*, and *IPP*.
- These genes are normally expressed *only* from the *paternal* allele. The maternal alleles are silenced through DNA methylation and histone modifications (imprinting).
- PWS can result from several genetic mechanisms affecting the paternal contribution of this region:
    1.  **Deletion:** Approximately 70% of cases involve a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. This is the most common cause.
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** About 25% of cases result from inheriting two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father (15q11-q13UPDmat). In this scenario, the individual has two maternal copies of the imprinted region, both of which are silenced, leading to a lack of expression of the paternally expressed genes.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** A small percentage (<5%) of cases are due to a defect in the imprinting process itself, where the paternal allele is inappropriately silenced (e.g., due to mutations in the imprinting control region).
- The clinical vignette describes a patient with classic PWS symptoms and FISH analysis showing the absence of the paternal *SNRPN* allele. This directly points to a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region as the underlying mechanism. The *SNRPN* gene is a key gene within this region whose absence from the paternal chromosome causes PWS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) A mutation in the maternal allele of the *UBE3A* gene, leading to Angelman syndrome.
    - **Incorrect.** Angelman syndrome (AS) is caused by a loss of function of the *UBE3A* gene, which is located in the same imprinted region (15q11-q13) as PWS genes. However, *UBE3A* is normally expressed *only* from the *maternal* allele in the brain. AS results from loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele (e.g., deletion, mutation, UPDpat, or imprinting defect). The patient's symptoms (hypotonia, developmental delay, hyperphagia) are more characteristic of PWS than AS (which typically presents with ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor, and severe developmental delay). The FISH finding specifically points to a paternal deletion, not a maternal one affecting *UBE3A*.
- (B) A deletion of the maternal allele of the *SNRPN* gene, leading to Prader-Willi syndrome.
    - **Incorrect.** PWS is caused by the loss of function of genes in the 15q11-q13 region that are normally expressed *only* from the *paternal* allele. A deletion of the *maternal* allele would not cause PWS because the paternal allele would still be active. Deletion of the maternal allele of *UBE3A* in this region causes Angelman syndrome. The FISH result explicitly shows the *paternal* allele is missing.
- (D) A translocation involving chromosome 21, leading to Down syndrome.
    - **Incorrect.** Down syndrome is caused by trisomy 21 (an extra copy of chromosome 21). The patient's karyotype was normal (46,XY), ruling out Down syndrome. The clinical features described (hypotonia, developmental delay, characteristic facies) overlap somewhat with Down syndrome, but the specific features (hyperphagia, small hands/feet, hypogonadism) and the FISH result are diagnostic for PWS.
- (E) A mutation in the *FGFR3* gene, leading to achondroplasia.
    - **Incorrect.** Achondroplasia is the most common form of dwarfism, caused by gain-of-function mutations in the *FGFR3* gene. It presents with short stature, rhizomelic shortening of limbs, macrocephaly, and characteristic facial features. The patient's symptoms (hypotonia, hyperphagia, developmental delay, specific facial features) are not consistent with achondroplasia. The genetic mechanism (imprinting) and specific gene locus (15q11-q13) are completely different.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Genomic imprinting results in monoallelic expression based on parental origin. Loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), while loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region causes Angelman syndrome (AS).
- Key differentials for PWS include Bardet-Biedl syndrome, Cohen syndrome, and other causes of developmental delay and hypotonia. Key differentials for AS include Rett syndrome, autism spectrum disorder, and other causes of severe developmental delay and ataxia. Understanding the specific genetic mechanism (paternal vs. maternal loss of function) and the associated genes (*SNRPN* vs. *UBE3A*) is crucial.

<a id='question-096'></a>

### Question 096: Prader-Willi Syndrome - Genetic Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. This can occur via paternal deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), or imprinting center defects. (Prader-Willi 综合征 (PWS) 是由 15q11.2-q13 区域父本表达基因功能丧失引起的，这是由于基因组印记造成的。这可以通过父本缺失、母本单亲二倍体 (UPD) 或印记中心缺陷发生。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his development. He was born full-term but had hypotonia and feeding difficulties in infancy, requiring tube feeding for the first 6 months. Since then, he has developed an insatiable appetite (hyperphagia) and has gained significant weight. His parents also report developmental delay, intellectual disability, short stature, and small hands and feet. Physical examination reveals obesity, characteristic facial features (narrow forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip), and hypogonadism (small penis, undescended testes). A karyotype analysis reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. Further investigation includes methylation analysis of the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15. The results show that the maternal allele of SNRPN is methylated, and the paternal allele is also methylated.

Which of the following genetic mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11.2-q13 region.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(C) Imprinting defect affecting the paternal allele of SNRPN.
(D) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(E) Maternal deletion of the 15q11.2-q13 region.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with history of neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, current hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, intellectual disability, short stature, small hands/feet, characteristic facial features, and hypogonadism. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidy (e.g., Down syndrome) or large chromosomal abnormalities.
- **Methylation Analysis:** This is the key diagnostic test for PWS. The SNRPN gene is located within the critical region (15q11.2-q13). In normal individuals, the paternal allele of SNRPN is unmethylated and expressed, while the maternal allele is methylated and silenced due to genomic imprinting. The finding that *both* the maternal and paternal alleles of SNRPN are methylated indicates a loss of expression of the paternal allele. This loss of paternal expression is the underlying cause of PWS.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region. This region contains several genes, including SNRPN (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), which is crucial for spliceosome assembly.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** A phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11.2-q13 region, the paternal allele is normally expressed, and the maternal allele is silenced (imprinted) through methylation.
- **Molecular Mechanism:**
    - **Normal:** Paternal allele (unmethylated, expressed) / Maternal allele (methylated, silenced).
    - **PWS:** Paternal allele (methylated, silenced) / Maternal allele (methylated, silenced).
- **Causes of PWS:**
    1.  **Paternal Deletion (approx. 70%):** Deletion of the critical region on the paternal chromosome 15. This removes the paternal allele, leaving only the methylated maternal allele.
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (approx. 25%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Both maternal copies are methylated and silenced, resulting in no expression of the paternal genes.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect (approx. 5%):** A defect in the imprinting center (IC1 or IC2) on the paternal chromosome 15 leads to aberrant methylation of the paternal allele, silencing it like the maternal allele.
- **Methylation Analysis Interpretation:** The finding that both alleles are methylated indicates that the paternal allele, which should be unmethylated and active, is silenced. This can happen if the paternal allele itself has a defect in the imprinting control region (ICR) leading to methylation, or if the individual has two maternal copies (UPD).
- **Connecting to the Question:** The vignette describes a patient with classic PWS symptoms and methylation analysis showing methylation of both alleles. This pattern specifically points to a defect in the imprinting mechanism itself on the paternal chromosome, causing the paternal allele to be incorrectly methylated and silenced.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Paternal deletion of the 15q11.2-q13 region:** This is the most common cause of PWS. However, in this scenario, methylation analysis would show the maternal allele methylated and the paternal allele *unmethylated* (because the paternal allele is physically absent). The vignette explicitly states *both* alleles are methylated. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (B) **Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15:** In maternal UPD, the individual has two maternal chromosomes 15 and no paternal chromosome 15. Both maternal alleles in the critical region are methylated and silenced. Methylation analysis would show methylation of both alleles, consistent with the vignette. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the *specific* finding of methylation of *both* alleles. While UPD fits the methylation pattern, an imprinting defect is also a possibility that fits this pattern. The key distinction is that UPD involves the *entire chromosome* origin, while an imprinting defect affects the *methylation status* of the paternal allele *in situ*. The vignette doesn't provide information to definitively distinguish between UPD and an imprinting defect based solely on the methylation pattern, but an imprinting defect directly explains the aberrant methylation of the paternal allele. Often, imprinting defects are considered a distinct category from deletion and UPD. Given the options, an imprinting defect (C) is a specific mechanism that directly explains the aberrant methylation pattern described.
- (D) **Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15:** This condition causes Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. In AS, the maternal allele is deleted or mutated, and the individual inherits two paternal copies (paternal UPD). The paternal alleles in the 15q11.2-q13 region are normally unmethylated and expressed, leading to overexpression of the maternal-like genes (like UBE3A) and loss of the paternal-like genes (like SNRPN). The methylation pattern in AS would show the maternal allele methylated (if present) and the paternal alleles unmethylated. This is the opposite of the PWS pattern and inconsistent with the vignette. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E) **Maternal deletion of the 15q11.2-q13 region:** This causes Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. The deletion removes the maternal allele, leaving the paternal allele as the only copy. The paternal allele is normally unmethylated and expressed. The methylation pattern would show the maternal allele absent/deleted and the paternal allele unmethylated. This is inconsistent with the vignette's finding of methylation of both alleles. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi syndrome results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The key diagnostic test is methylation analysis, which distinguishes PWS (paternal allele methylated) from Angelman syndrome (maternal allele methylated). PWS can be caused by paternal deletion, maternal UPD, or an imprinting defect. Angelman syndrome is caused by maternal deletion, paternal UPD, or certain mutations in the maternal UBE3A gene. The specific methylation pattern (both alleles methylated) points towards either maternal UPD or an imprinting defect affecting the paternal allele. An imprinting defect is a specific mechanism where the paternal allele itself is aberrantly methylated.

**Differentials:**
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** Caused by loss of function of the maternal UBE3A gene. Features include severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor, and characteristic facial features. Methylation analysis shows methylation of the maternal allele and unmethylation of the paternal alleles.
- **Maternal Deletion 15q11.2-q13:** Causes AS.
- **Paternal Deletion 15q11.2-q13:** Causes PWS.
- **Maternal UPD 15:** Causes PWS.
- **Paternal UPD 15:** Causes AS.

<a id='question-097'></a>

### Question 097: Angelman Syndrome: Paternal 15q11-q13 Imprinted/Silenced with Maternal UBE3A Deletion or Paternal UPD - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy (UPD) leading to loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene, causing Angelman syndrome (AS). (基因组印记和单亲二体畸 (UPD) 导致 *UBE3A* 基因功能丧失，引起 Angelman 综合征 (AS))

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter. Her speech is minimal, and she exhibits microcephaly and characteristic jerky movements. Her parents report that she was born full-term without complications. Her father is 45 years old. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region shows a pattern consistent with paternal inheritance for both alleles. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the *UBE3A* gene shows that the maternal allele is deleted. The paternal allele is present and intact.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region encompassing the *UBE3A* gene.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(C) Imprinting defect leading to biallelic expression of the paternally inherited *UBE3A* gene.
(D) Mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene that results in a non-functional protein.
(E) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Clinical Presentation:** Developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet"), minimal speech, microcephaly, jerky movements. These are classic features of Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Age:** 3 years old, consistent with the typical onset and progression of AS symptoms.
- **Family History:** Father is 45 years old, increasing the risk of errors in meiosis leading to UPD.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX, ruling out large chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 15 or large deletions/translocations detectable by standard karyotyping.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Shows a pattern consistent with paternal inheritance for *both* alleles at the 15q11-q13 locus. In normal individuals, the maternal allele is unmethylated (active), and the paternal allele is methylated (silent) due to genomic imprinting. A pattern consistent with paternal inheritance for both alleles means both alleles are methylated (silent). This is the hallmark of AS caused by maternal deletion or paternal UPD.
- **FISH Analysis:** Shows deletion of the maternal *UBE3A* allele. The paternal allele is intact.

**Deduction:** The patient has clinical features of AS. The methylation analysis indicates that both copies of the *UBE3A* gene are silenced (methylated paternally). The FISH analysis confirms the maternal *UBE3A* gene is deleted. Therefore, the patient lacks a functional maternal copy of *UBE3A*, and the paternal copy is silenced by imprinting. This combination leads to the loss of *UBE3A* function, causing AS. The mechanism responsible for the loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele is paternal UPD, where the patient inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from her father.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Angelman syndrome (AS) is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that gene expression depends on the parent of origin. Normally, the maternal *UBE3A* allele is expressed in the brain, while the paternal allele is silenced by methylation. The *UBE3A* gene encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation. Loss of maternal *UBE3A* function leads to the characteristic AS phenotype.

Several genetic mechanisms can cause AS:
1.  **Maternal deletion of 15q11-q13:** This is the most common cause (~70%), removing the active maternal *UBE3A* allele.
2.  **Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15:** The patient inherits both copies of chromosome 15 from the father. Both copies carry the paternally imprinted, methylated, and thus silenced *UBE3A* allele. This accounts for ~3-7% of cases.
3.  **Imprinting defect:** A defect in the imprinting center (IC1) on the maternal chromosome 15 leads to methylation of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, silencing it. This accounts for ~3-7% of cases.
4.  **Mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene:** A mutation renders the maternal *UBE3A* protein non-functional. This accounts for ~10-15% of cases.

In this specific case:
- The clinical presentation is classic AS.
- Methylation analysis shows both alleles are methylated (paternally imprinted pattern). This indicates that the maternal allele is silenced, either by deletion, UPD, or an imprinting defect.
- FISH analysis confirms a deletion of the maternal *UBE3A* allele.
- Since the methylation pattern shows both alleles are methylated, and the maternal allele is deleted, the patient must have inherited both chromosome 15s from her father (paternal UPD) to have two methylated (silent) paternal alleles. The deletion of the maternal allele is a consequence of the UPD event, not the primary cause of the methylated pattern. The key finding is the methylation pattern (both alleles methylated) combined with the FISH result (maternal deletion). This combination points strongly to paternal UPD as the underlying mechanism. If it were just a maternal deletion, the methylation pattern would show the paternal allele methylated and the maternal allele unmethylated (if present) or absent. The fact that *both* alleles are methylated indicates that the patient inherited both chromosomes 15 from the father.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region encompassing the *UBE3A* gene causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), not Angelman syndrome. PWS presents with hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia, obesity, and developmental delay. The methylation pattern in PWS would show methylation of both alleles, but this would be due to loss of the *paternal* allele, not the maternal one. The clinical vignette describes AS, not PWS.
- (B): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 would result in the patient inheriting both copies of chromosome 15 from the mother. Both copies would carry the unmethylated, active maternal *UBE3A* allele. This would lead to *overexpression* of *UBE3A* and is not associated with Angelman syndrome. It is associated with Prader-Willi syndrome if the maternal chromosome 15 has a deletion of the paternal-specific region.
- (C): An imprinting defect leading to biallelic expression of the paternally inherited *UBE3A* gene is incorrect. An imprinting defect in AS involves the maternal chromosome 15, leading to methylation of the *maternal* *UBE3A* allele, silencing it. The paternal allele remains methylated and silenced. This scenario would result in loss of the maternal *UBE3A* function, but the methylation pattern would show the paternal allele methylated and the maternal allele methylated (due to the defect), consistent with the vignette. However, the FISH result shows a maternal deletion, making an imprinting defect less likely as the primary mechanism, although it could coexist. More importantly, the question asks for the *best* explanation given *all* the data. The combination of methylation pattern (both methylated) and maternal deletion strongly points to paternal UPD. If the primary mechanism were an imprinting defect, the maternal allele would still be present (though methylated), which contradicts the FISH finding of maternal deletion.
- (D): A mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene that results in a non-functional protein would cause AS. However, in this case, the methylation analysis shows that both alleles are methylated (paternally imprinted pattern). If the maternal allele were present but mutated, it would typically be unmethylated (active) unless there was a co-existing imprinting defect. The FISH result also shows the maternal allele is deleted, contradicting the presence of a mutated maternal allele.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene. This can occur via maternal deletion, paternal UPD, imprinting defect, or maternal *UBE3A* mutation. The key to diagnosis often lies in combining clinical features with molecular testing (methylation analysis, FISH, sequencing). Paternal UPD (15q11-q13) results in two methylated (silent) paternal copies of *UBE3A*, leading to AS. This is differentiated from PWS, which results from loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the same region (e.g., maternal deletion, paternal UPD). Remember the classic AS features (ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor, developmental delay) and the molecular basis involving maternal *UBE3A* loss.

<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome: Loss of 11p15 Imprinting, IGF2 Overexpression, Omphalocele, Macroglossia, and Wilms Tumor - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of genomic imprinting, specifically the loss of imprinting at the 11p15 locus, leading to overexpression of imprinted genes like IGF2, and its association with Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome (BWS). It also assesses the clinical manifestations of BWS and the associated cancer risk. (本题考察基因组印记，特别是11p15位点印记丢失，导致印记基因如IGF2过度表达，以及其与贝克维德曼综合征（BWS）的关联。同时也考察BWS的临床表现和相关的癌症风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old male infant, born at term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery to a 32-year-old primigravida mother, is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU). The mother reports no significant prenatal history, no known family history of congenital anomalies, and no history of assisted reproductive technologies. On examination, the infant exhibits macroglossia (tongue protruding from the mouth), an omphalocele containing loops of bowel, and apparent macrosomia (birth weight 5.2 kg, >95th percentile). Abdominal ultrasound reveals bilateral nephromegaly. Karyotype analysis is normal (46,XY). A subsequent follow-up at 6 months shows continued overgrowth (height and weight >95th percentile), ear creases, and a nevus flammeus on the glabella. At 2 years of age, the child is diagnosed with a Wilms tumor in the left kidney, confirmed by biopsy. Genetic testing reveals a paternally inherited deletion encompassing the 11p15.5 region, including the H19/IGF2 imprinted domain.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the development of the Wilms tumor in this patient?

(A) Loss of function mutation in the *TP53* tumor suppressor gene, leading to impaired DNA repair and genomic instability.
(B) Loss of maternal imprinting control at the 11p15 locus, resulting in biallelic expression of the *H19* oncogene.
(C) Loss of paternal imprinting control at the 11p15 locus, leading to overexpression of the *IGF2* proto-oncogene.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the *WT1* gene, causing constitutive activation of the Wnt signaling pathway.
(E) Somatic mutation in the *SMARCB1* gene, leading to loss of SWI/SNF chromatin remodeling complex function.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Macrosomia, macroglossia, omphalocele, ear creases, nevus flammeus, bilateral nephromegaly, and Wilms tumor. These are classic features of Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome (BWS).
*   **Genetic Finding:** Paternally inherited deletion encompassing the 11p15.5 region, including the H19/IGF2 imprinted domain. This points towards a genetic defect involving genomic imprinting.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the Wilms tumor development in this specific context (BWS with 11p15 deletion).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** The 11p15.5 region contains two key imprinted genes: *H19* (maternally expressed) and *IGF2* (paternally expressed). Imprinting involves epigenetic modifications (DNA methylation, histone modifications) that silence one allele (usually the paternal allele for *H19* and the maternal allele for *IGF2*) based on parental origin.
*   **BWS Pathogenesis:** BWS can arise from several genetic mechanisms affecting this region, including:
    *   Paternal deletion of 11p15.5 (as in this case): Loss of the normally silenced paternal *H19* allele and loss of the normally expressed paternal *IGF2* allele. However, the maternal *IGF2* allele is also normally silenced by imprinting. The deletion removes the paternal *H19* gene, which normally acts as a negative regulator of *IGF2*. This leads to loss of *H19* repression, allowing the maternal *IGF2* allele to be expressed at higher levels than normal (biallelic expression).
    *   Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 11p15.5: The child inherits two copies of the maternal chromosome 11, resulting in loss of the paternal *IGF2* allele and retention of the maternal *H19* allele. This also leads to overexpression of *IGF2* (biallelic expression).
    *   Imprinting center defects (IC1, IC2): Mutations in these regulatory regions can disrupt the silencing of either *H19* or *IGF2*, leading to aberrant expression.
    *   Hypomethylation of the *H19/IGF2* imprinting control region (ICR): This can lead to biallelic expression of both *H19* and *IGF2*.
*   **IGF2 and Cancer:** *IGF2* (Insulin-like Growth Factor 2) is a potent mitogen and growth factor. Its overexpression is strongly implicated in the development of several cancers, including Wilms tumor, hepatoblastoma, and rhabdomyosarcoma, which are associated with BWS. The overexpression of *IGF2* due to loss of imprinting at 11p15 drives cell proliferation and contributes to tumorigenesis.
*   **Specific Mechanism in this Case:** The patient has a paternal deletion of 11p15.5. This removes the paternal copy of both *H19* and *IGF2*. The maternal copy of *H19* remains, but the maternal copy of *IGF2* is normally silenced by imprinting. However, the loss of the paternal *H19* gene removes its repressive effect on the maternal *IGF2* gene, leading to its inappropriate activation and overexpression. This biallelic expression of *IGF2* is the key driver for the increased cancer risk, specifically Wilms tumor, in this patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): *TP53* is a crucial tumor suppressor gene involved in DNA repair and apoptosis. Loss-of-function mutations in *TP53* are common in many cancers (e.g., Li-Fraumeni syndrome), leading to genomic instability. While *TP53* mutations can occur in Wilms tumor, they are not the primary underlying mechanism specifically linked to the 11p15 imprinting defect in BWS. BWS is primarily driven by *IGF2* overexpression.
- (B): Loss of maternal imprinting control at the 11p15 locus would lead to expression of the maternal *H19* allele. While *H19* is an oncogene and its overexpression can contribute to tumorigenesis, the primary driver of Wilms tumor in BWS associated with 11p15 defects is the overexpression of *IGF2*. Furthermore, the vignette describes a *paternal* deletion, which affects the paternal alleles and leads to overexpression of the maternal *IGF2* allele, not the maternal *H19* allele.
- (D): *WT1* (Wilms Tumor 1) is a tumor suppressor gene frequently mutated or deleted in Wilms tumor. Gain-of-function mutations are not typical for *WT1* in this context; loss-of-function is the mechanism. Moreover, while *WT1* mutations are involved in Wilms tumor development, the question specifically asks for the mechanism related to the underlying BWS genetic defect (11p15 deletion), which is *IGF2* overexpression.
- (E): *SMARCB1* (also known as *INI1*) encodes a subunit of the SWI/SNF chromatin remodeling complex. Loss-of-function mutations in *SMARCB1* are characteristic of rhabdoid tumors, not typically Wilms tumor, although they can occur rarely. This mechanism is unrelated to the 11p15 imprinting defect described in the vignette.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome is caused by defects in genomic imprinting at the 11p15 locus, leading to overexpression of the *IGF2* proto-oncogene. This overexpression increases the risk of embryonal tumors, particularly Wilms tumor. Key features include macrosomia, macroglossia, omphalocele, and hemihypertrophy. Remember the specific genes involved (*H19*, *IGF2*) and the mechanism (loss of imprinting leading to *IGF2* overexpression). Differentiate BWS from other overgrowth syndromes like Sotos syndrome or Weaver syndrome, which have different genetic causes and clinical features. Also, differentiate the molecular mechanism of BWS from other causes of Wilms tumor, such as *WT1* mutations or aniridia.

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Silver-Russell Syndrome: Hypomethylation of 11p15 or Maternal UPD7 Leading to Severe Growth Retardation and Asymmetry - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanisms underlying Silver-Russell Syndrome (SRS), specifically focusing on the role of genomic imprinting, hypomethylation of the H19/IGF2 locus on chromosome 11p15, and maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7, and how these genetic events lead to the characteristic clinical features of SRS. (考察银-罗素综合征 (SRS) 的分子机制，特别是 11p15 染色体上 H19/IGF2 基因座的基因组印记、低甲基化以及 7 号染色体母系单亲二体 (UPD) 的作用，以及这些遗传事件如何导致 SRS 的特征性临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl is brought to the pediatrician by her parents due to concerns about her growth. She was born via uncomplicated vaginal delivery at 34 weeks gestation, weighing 1.8 kg (below the 3rd percentile). Since birth, her growth has been consistently poor, with a current height of 78 cm (below the 1st percentile) and weight of 11 kg (below the 1st percentile). Her parents are of average height and weight. Physical examination reveals relative macrocephaly (head circumference at the 10th percentile), a triangular facial appearance with a prominent forehead and pointed chin, body asymmetry (left limb shorter than the right), and clinodactyly of the fifth finger. Her developmental milestones are within normal limits for her age. A complete blood count, basic metabolic panel, thyroid function tests, and celiac disease screening are all normal. A chromosomal microarray analysis is normal. Given the clinical presentation, further genetic testing is pursued. Methylation analysis of the H19/IGF2 imprinted region on chromosome 11p15 reveals decreased methylation in the patient's DNA compared to her mother's DNA.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the SNRPN gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, leading to loss of imprinting control elements (ICEs) and subsequent biallelic expression of maternal SNRPN.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7, resulting in the absence of paternally expressed genes on 7q31, including the IMP2/MAST2 imprinted domain.
(C) Hypomethylation of the H19/IGF2 imprinted region on chromosome 11p15, leading to increased expression of H19 and decreased expression of IGF2.
(D) Paternal deletion of the KCNQ1OT1 gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, resulting in loss of the antisense transcript that normally silences the maternal KCNQ1 gene.
(E) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 11, resulting in the absence of paternally expressed genes on 11p15, including the H19/IGF2 imprinted domain and the paternally expressed IGF2 gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR) and postnatal growth failure (height and weight <1st percentile), relative macrocephaly, triangular face, body asymmetry, clinodactyly. These are classic features of Silver-Russell Syndrome (SRS).
*   **Normal Investigations:** Normal CBC, BMP, thyroid function, celiac screening, and chromosomal microarray rule out common causes of growth failure like malnutrition, endocrine disorders, chromosomal abnormalities (like Turner syndrome), and common genetic syndromes detectable by microarray.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Methylation analysis of the H19/IGF2 locus on 11p15 shows decreased methylation in the patient compared to her mother. This is a key finding. The H19/IGF2 locus is an imprinted region on chromosome 11p15. In normal individuals, the paternal allele is unmethylated, allowing expression of IGF2, while the maternal allele is heavily methylated, silencing H19 and IGF2. Decreased methylation in the patient suggests loss of imprinting on the maternal allele.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Silver-Russell Syndrome (SRS):** A disorder characterized by prenatal and postnatal growth retardation, relative macrocephaly, triangular facies, body asymmetry, and feeding difficulties. It is genetically heterogeneous.
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This is often regulated by DNA methylation.
*   **11p15 Imprinted Region:** Contains two major imprinted domains:
    *   **IGF2/H19 domain:** Paternally expressed IGF2 (Insulin-like Growth Factor 2) is a growth promoter. Maternally expressed H19 is a non-coding RNA involved in growth regulation (often acting as a tumor suppressor). In normal development, the paternal allele is unmethylated (IGF2 expressed, H19 silenced), and the maternal allele is heavily methylated (IGF2 silenced, H19 silenced).
    *   **KCNQ1OT1/KCNQ1 domain:** Paternally expressed KCNQ1OT1 is an antisense transcript that silences the maternal KCNQ1 gene.
*   **Molecular Mechanism in SRS (Hypomethylation of 11p15):** In about 7-10% of SRS cases, there is hypomethylation of the maternal allele at the H19/IGF2 locus. This leads to:
    *   **Increased expression of H19:** H19 acts as a growth inhibitor.
    *   **Decreased expression of IGF2:** IGF2 is a growth promoter.
    *   The net effect is reduced growth, explaining the severe growth retardation seen in the patient.
*   **Methylation Analysis:** The finding of decreased methylation in the patient's DNA compared to her mother's DNA specifically points to a defect in the methylation pattern of the maternal allele at the 11p15 locus. This is consistent with the hypomethylation mechanism of SRS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Paternal deletion of the SNRPN gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, leading to loss of imprinting control elements (ICEs) and subsequent biallelic expression of maternal SNRPN.** This describes the mechanism of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS). PWS involves loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region, including SNRPN. The clinical features (hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia/obesity later, developmental delay, short stature) are distinct from SRS. The genetic locus (15q11-q13) and the gene involved (SNRPN) are incorrect for SRS.
*   **(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 7, resulting in the absence of paternally expressed genes on 7q31, including the IMP2/MAST2 imprinted domain.** Maternal UPD7 causes Russell-Silver Syndrome type 2. The IMP2/MAST2 genes are paternally expressed and involved in placental development and growth. Loss of paternal contribution leads to IUGR and SRS features. While this *is* a cause of SRS, the vignette specifically mentions *decreased methylation* at the 11p15 locus, pointing towards a defect at that specific location, not UPD7. The question asks for the mechanism *most likely* responsible given the specific lab finding.
*   **(D) Paternal deletion of the KCNQ1OT1 gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, resulting in loss of the antisense transcript that normally silences the maternal KCNQ1 gene.** This describes the mechanism of Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome (BWS). BWS involves overexpression of imprinted genes on chromosome 11p15, particularly IGF2, due to loss of imprinting control. The clinical features (macrosomia, macroglossia, omphalocele, hemihypertrophy) are the opposite of SRS. The genetic locus (15q11-q13) and the gene involved (KCNQ1OT1) are incorrect for SRS.
*   **(E) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 11, resulting in the absence of paternally expressed genes on 11p15, including the H19/IGF2 imprinted domain and the paternally expressed IGF2 gene.** Maternal UPD11 is another cause of SRS. It leads to the absence of the paternal contribution at the 11p15 locus, resulting in biallelic expression of the maternal H19 and silencing of the paternal IGF2. This would also cause SRS. However, the vignette specifically reports *decreased methylation* at the 11p15 locus, which is characteristic of the hypomethylation mechanism (Option C), not UPD11. UPD11 would typically show normal methylation patterns but lack the paternal allele.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Silver-Russell Syndrome (SRS) is caused by defects in the 11p15 imprinted region or maternal UPD7. The most common mechanism involves hypomethylation of the maternal allele at the H19/IGF2 locus, leading to increased H19 expression and decreased IGF2 expression, resulting in growth restriction. Differentiating SRS from other growth disorders like PWS (15q11-q13 deletion, SNRPN loss) and BWS (11p15 overexpression, KCNQ1OT1/IGF2) is crucial, as is recognizing the different genetic causes of SRS (hypomethylation 11p15, maternal UPD7, paternal UPD11, mutations in SRSF2, IGF2, etc.). The specific finding of decreased methylation at 11p15 points directly to the mechanism described in option C.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: X-Chromosome Inactivation (Lyonization)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: X-chromosome inactivation (Lyonization) ensures dosage compensation between males (XY) and females (XX) by randomly inactivating one X chromosome in each female somatic cell early in embryonic development. This process involves epigenetic modifications, primarily DNA methylation and histone modifications, leading to transcriptional silencing of the inactive X chromosome. Understanding the molecular mechanisms and consequences of X-inactivation is crucial. (X染色体失活（Lyonization）确保雄性（XY）和雌性（XX）之间剂量补偿，通过在每个雌性体细胞早期胚胎发育中随机失活一条X染色体来实现。这个过程涉及表观遗传修饰，主要是DNA甲基化和组蛋白修饰，导致非活化X染色体的转录沉默。理解X失活的分子机制和后果至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 4-year-old girl presents with developmental delay, short stature, and distinctive facial features including a broad forehead, hypertelorism, and epicanthal folds. Her mother has similar, though milder, features. Her father is unaffected. Karyotyping reveals a 46,XX karyotype. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the XIST gene (a key non-coding RNA involved in X-inactivation) shows that in a large proportion (>90%) of her peripheral blood leukocytes, only one X chromosome exhibits strong XIST signal, indicating proper X-inactivation. However, analysis of skin fibroblasts reveals that approximately 50% of cells show strong XIST signal on only one X chromosome, while the remaining 50% show strong XIST signal on *both* X chromosomes, or no strong signal on either. Furthermore, quantitative PCR analysis of transcripts from genes located on the X chromosome (e.g., *PGK*, phosphoglycerate kinase) shows significantly higher levels of expression in the patient's skin fibroblasts compared to control fibroblasts from unaffected females. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's phenotype and the observed laboratory findings?

(A) A mutation in the *XIST* gene prevents its proper function, leading to biallelic expression of X-linked genes in all cells.
(B) A mutation in the *XPC* gene (involved in nucleotide excision repair) leads to increased mutation rates on the X chromosome, causing a high burden of deleterious mutations that overwhelm the compensatory mechanisms.
(C) A mutation in the *SOX9* gene (involved in sex determination) leads to incomplete suppression of the SRY gene on the X chromosome, resulting in mosaicism for male-determining features.
(D) A mutation in the *AR* gene (androgen receptor) leads to androgen insensitivity syndrome, causing feminization in an XY individual, but this patient is XX.
(E) A mutation in the *CHD8* gene (chromodomain helicase DNA binding protein 8), which encodes a component of the NuRD complex involved in chromatin remodeling and X-inactivation, leads to impaired X-inactivation in some cell lineages.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 4-year-old girl with developmental delay, short stature, and dysmorphic features (broad forehead, hypertelorism, epicanthal folds). Mother has similar milder features. Father is unaffected. This suggests a genetic disorder, potentially X-linked or autosomal, with variable expressivity or mosaicism. The maternal inheritance pattern is suggestive but not definitive.
- **Karyotype:** 46,XX confirms the patient is female.
- **FISH (XIST):** In blood leukocytes (>90% cells), only one X chromosome shows strong XIST signal, indicating normal X-inactivation (Lyonization). This rules out a global failure of X-inactivation affecting all cell types.
- **Fibroblasts (XIST):** In skin fibroblasts (50% cells), strong XIST signal on only one X chromosome (normal), 50% show strong signal on *both* X chromosomes, or no strong signal on either. This indicates *impaired* or *incomplete* X-inactivation specifically in this cell lineage.
- **Gene Expression (X-linked):** *PGK* transcripts (X-linked gene) are significantly higher in skin fibroblasts compared to controls, consistent with biallelic expression of X-linked genes due to failed inactivation.
- **Connecting Clues:** The combination of normal X-inactivation in blood cells, impaired X-inactivation in skin fibroblasts, and elevated expression of X-linked genes in fibroblasts points to a cell-lineage specific defect in the X-inactivation process. The patient's phenotype (developmental delay, dysmorphic features) is consistent with overexpression of X-linked genes in certain tissues.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **X-Chromosome Inactivation (Lyonization):** Occurs early in female embryonic development (blastocyst stage). One X chromosome is randomly chosen in each cell and silenced epigenetically. This involves:
    - Coating the X chromosome with XIST RNA.
    - Recruitment of chromatin-modifying complexes (e.g., NuRD complex containing CHD8, histone deacetylases, methyltransferases).
    - Extensive DNA methylation of CpG islands in promoter regions.
    - Formation of repressive chromatin structures (heterochromatin).
- **Dosage Compensation:** Ensures that females (XX) do not have twice the gene product from X-linked genes compared to males (XY).
- **Mechanism in the Vignette:** The patient exhibits mosaicism for X-inactivation. Blood cells show normal inactivation, while skin fibroblasts show significant failure of inactivation (biallelic expression). This suggests a defect in the machinery responsible for initiating or maintaining X-inactivation, specifically affecting fibroblast lineage.
- **CHD8 Gene:** Encodes chromodomain helicase DNA binding protein 8, a core component of the NuRD complex. The NuRD complex is crucial for histone deacetylation and chromatin compaction, playing a key role in X-inactivation. Mutations in *CHD8* are known to cause X-linked dominant disorders characterized by mosaic X-inactivation, leading to overexpression of X-linked genes in affected tissues and resulting phenotypes like Sotos syndrome (overgrowth) or Noonan syndrome-like features (short stature, developmental delay, dysmorphism). The patient's phenotype and the specific pattern of mosaic X-inactivation (normal in blood, impaired in fibroblasts) are highly consistent with a *CHD8* mutation affecting the NuRD complex function in certain cell types.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A mutation in *XIST* preventing its function would likely lead to a *global* failure of X-inactivation, affecting *all* cell types. The vignette clearly shows normal X-inactivation in blood cells, ruling out a complete loss-of-function *XIST* mutation. While *XIST* mutations can cause X-inactivation defects, they typically result in more widespread failure, not the cell-lineage specific mosaicism seen here.
- (B): *XPC* is involved in nucleotide excision repair (NER) of DNA damage, particularly UV-induced damage. Mutations in *XPC* cause Xeroderma Pigmentosum (XP), characterized by extreme sun sensitivity and a high risk of skin cancer. While XP can involve increased mutation rates, it doesn't directly explain the specific defect in X-inactivation machinery or the mosaic pattern observed. The patient's phenotype is also not typical for XP.
- (C): *SOX9* is involved in sex determination, typically expressed in males. Mutations in *SOX9* can cause sex reversal (XY individuals developing as females). The SRY gene is on the Y chromosome. While SRY can be translocated to the X chromosome, leading to XX males, this patient is female (XX). Furthermore, mutations in *SOX9* wouldn't directly explain the mosaic X-inactivation pattern or the specific biochemical findings related to X-linked gene expression.
- (D): The *AR* gene encodes the androgen receptor. Mutations cause Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS). In complete AIS (CAIS), XY individuals are phenotypically female due to inability to respond to androgens. This patient is XX, so *AR* mutations are irrelevant to her genetic cause.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the process of X-chromosome inactivation (Lyonization) and its importance for dosage compensation. Recognize that defects in the X-inactivation machinery (e.g., mutations in *XIST*, *CHD8*, *LIN28B*) can lead to mosaicism, where some cells fail to inactivate an X chromosome, resulting in overexpression of X-linked genes and specific clinical phenotypes.
- **Differentials:** Consider other causes of developmental delay and dysmorphic features. X-linked disorders with variable expressivity or skewed X-inactivation can sometimes mimic mosaic X-inactivation defects, but the specific pattern of normal inactivation in one cell type (blood) and failure in another (fibroblasts) strongly points to a defect in the inactivation process itself, like a *CHD8* mutation. Other X-linked disorders like Rett syndrome (MECP2) or Fragile X syndrome (FMR1) have different underlying mechanisms and clinical presentations.

---
